## Prioritize money pages over blog content in ecommerce SEO
Use when: Deciding how to allocate SEO budget and content effort across collection/product pages versus a blog for a Shopify store.
- List your transactional 'money pages': product and collection/category pages that correspond to keywords a shopper would use when ready to buy.
- Rank each candidate by whether a searcher using that keyword is likely to purchase now (e.g. 'natural face wash' vs 'why should I use natural face wash').
- Prioritize building out and ranking collection pages for the highest-intent keywords first; treat informational blog posts as secondary or skip them.
- Measure direct and assisted conversions from blog content in this store; the source's 0%-2% range is not a universal benchmark.
- For a brand struggling with this, the source's example was building roughly 40 new collection pages in the first 3-4 months, which coincided with organic search revenue rising from ~$2K/month to over $10K/month (case example, not a benchmark).
- Measure success by organic search revenue, not traffic growth, since traffic without conversions is a sunk cost.
Caveat: The source's stance is strongly anti-blog; some stores with high-consideration products may still benefit from informational content, so treat this as a prioritization rule, not an absolute ban.
Caveat: Case-study revenue jumps are single examples, not guaranteed outcomes.

Tags: search, product-selection, merchandising, conversion-optimization, revenue-growth, shopify
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

---

## Simplify technical SEO to four essentials
Use when: Evaluating a technical SEO audit or agency pitch and deciding which fixes are actually worth doing on a store.
- Confirm your key pages are crawlable and indexed properly — the source says if Google cannot find key pages, nothing else matters.
- Fix broken links (404s), since they hurt user experience and rankings.
- Ensure good mobile and desktop UX: fast enough, easy to navigate, not confusing.
- Establish clear hierarchy so Google and users understand which pages (e.g. collections) are most important.
- Ignore most other technical items pushed by agencies: meta descriptions have not been a ranking factor since 2014 (per source), and minor speed fixes are helpful but not necessary.
- If an agency/freelancer pitches endless technical fixes, the source advises caution — treat that as a warning sign rather than a requirement.
Caveat: The source's opinion that meta descriptions and minor speed fixes don't matter is presented as its own view; actual importance can vary and Google's documented rankings do involve page experience signals.
Caveat: Hierarchy still matters and should be verified with internal linking structure.

Tags: search, technical-seo, site-architecture, indexation, page-experience
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

---

## Evaluate relevant existing-page placements for store referrals
Use when: Planning a backlink acquisition campaign or budget and choosing between link insertion and guest posting for a store.
- Compile a list of sites in your niche that accept guest posts or link insertions.
- Find contact information using a scraping/contact tool like hunter.io (verify current tool).
- Ask about editorial relevance and sponsorship terms; qualify any paid placement with sponsored or nofollow.
- Evaluate candidate pages on domain rating, domain traffic, page traffic, and relevance before buying.
- Prefer link insertions over guest posts: the source reports insertions cost roughly 20-30% less on average because the owner just tweaks existing content rather than publishing a new piece.
- Prefer pages that already have established traffic, since a guest post has no guarantee of traffic while an inserted link on a live page already benefits from that page's traffic.
- Alternatively, use link marketplaces, but expect them to be roughly 20-60% more expensive than direct outreach because they handle the outreach for you — reach out directly if cash-constrained.
Caveat: Prices ($100-$400 average per insertion, per source) are the presenter's current spend, not a fixed market rate.
Caveat: The '20-30% cheaper' and '20-60% marketplace markup' figures are source-specific observations; verify current pricing before budgeting.
Caveat: Editorial qualification: paid placements must use rel="sponsored" or rel="nofollow"; do not buy unqualified links to manipulate rankings. Assess sponsored placements by relevant referral traffic and commercial value, not promised ranking credit.

Tags: search, link-building, acquisition, outreach, backlinks
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

---

## Aim for a modest number of quality backlinks, not thousands
Use when: Setting link-building targets or evaluating a cheap SEO offer that promises large volumes of links.
- Recognize that sites with thousands of backlinks still often fail to rank number one, so volume alone is not the goal.
- Target roughly 10-20 relevant, authoritative links for many keywords (source's heuristic, not a fixed rule).
- Avoid cheap SEO services, agencies and freelancers that flood your site with garbage links, since these can hurt rather than help rankings.
- Spend link budget on relevance, authority and traffic rather than sheer count.
- Blend link insertions with other methods (digital PR, relationship-based outreach) rather than relying on one source alone.
Caveat: The '10-20 backlinks' figure is a heuristic the presenter gives for 'a lot of keywords,' not an established threshold; different niches and competitiveness levels vary.
Caveat: Quality and relevance matter more than any exact number.
Caveat: Editorial qualification: paid placements must use rel="sponsored" or rel="nofollow"; do not buy unqualified links to manipulate rankings. Assess sponsored placements by relevant referral traffic and commercial value, not promised ranking credit.

Tags: search, link-building, backlinks, acquisition, authority
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

---

## Apply the 80/20 rule to SEO effort and revenue
Use when: Allocating limited SEO time or budget across reporting, technical fixes, content and link building for a store.
- Identify the two highest-leverage activities: building/optimizing transactional money pages and acquiring quality backlinks.
- Concentrate most of your impact (the source estimates ~20% of time) on these two activities.
- Accept that reporting, technical SEO, blog content and internal linking may consume ~80% of time yet drive the minority of revenue growth.
- Stop trying to do everything: explicitly deprioritize low-revenue SEO tasks during limited-capacity periods.
- Track organic search revenue as the primary success metric and reallocate toward whichever of the two levers is underfunded.
Caveat: 80/20 is a heuristic framing from the source, not a measured calculation; actual effort/revenue split varies by store.
Caveat: The source is a marketer promoting its own approach; treat the ratio as an orientation, not a guarantee.

Tags: search, prioritization, revenue-growth, money-pages, link-building
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

---

## Track organic search revenue as the primary SEO KPI
Use when: Reviewing an SEO agency's monthly reporting or deciding whether SEO spend is working for your store.
- Set organic search revenue as the number one KPI for all SEO work.
- Ignore or discount reporting focused on traffic growth, blog impressions and keyword rankings that don't correspond to revenue.
- Benchmark against paid spend: the source cites a brand paying two agencies ~$5K/month each (~$120K/year) that never exceeded ~$2K/month in organic revenue — a net-negative spend.
- If organic revenue isn't increasing after a reasonable period, treat the SEO program as not working and change approach.
- Use paid-search/CPC signals (e.g. Google Ads Keyword Planner data) to validate whether a keyword is commercial before investing.
Caveat: The revenue-vs-spend example is one case study, not a universal benchmark; timelines for SEO results vary.
Caveat: Some early-stage SEO work legitimately takes time before revenue appears, so a short evaluation window can be misleading.

Tags: search, measurement, analytics, revenue-growth, agency-management
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

## Use a lean SEO tool stack: Semrush, Google Keyword Planner, Screaming Frog
Use when: Choosing or trimming SEO tool subscriptions and setting up keyword and technical research for a store.
- Use Semrush (or an equivalent all-in-one) for competitor analysis, keyword research, backlink gap analysis and copycat strategies.
- Use Google Ads Keyword Planner as a free keyword research tool you likely already have access to if you run Google Ads.
- Note that Keyword Planner gives search-volume ranges, not exact numbers: fine for small keywords, but the gap between 10K and 100K is substantial, so treat large-range keywords cautiously.
- Use average CPC (bottom-of-page bid) as a proxy for commercial intent where paid tools give keyword difficulty, since Keyword Planner does not provide difficulty.
- Use Screaming Frog for technical crawls (404s, missing H1s/H2s, titles, meta descriptions, URLs, canonicals); it is free for up to 500 URLs, so small sites can use it free.
- Verify current pricing and free-tier limits before committing, as tools change over time.
Caveat: Semrush pricing/features and Screaming Frog's free URL limit change over time; verify the current interface.
Caveat: Keyword Planner volume ranges are approximations, and CPC is a proxy for intent, not a difficulty score.

Tags: search, seo-tools, keyword-research, semrush, screaming-frog, technical-seo
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

## Build standout content and relationships for free organic links
Use when: Deciding whether to invest in digital PR and link-worthy content versus paid link acquisition for a store.
- If pursuing free/organic links, expand a roster of journalists and bloggers who cover your niche and build relationships over time.
- Create genuinely standout content or assets (source calls this the hardest and least scalable link method) that others choose to link to.
- Expect to burn through many pieces of content before earning the first organic link.
- Budget realistically: the source estimates earning links via digital PR costs around $5K/month; if you're earning links purely organically, it's typically because you're already a high-domain-authority brand (DR 70-80).
- Weigh organic link building against cheaper link insertions, which the source prefers for most brands.
Caveat: The $5K/month digital PR figure and DR 70-80 claim are the presenter's observations, not established benchmarks.
Caveat: Organic link building is slow and less scalable; do not expect near-term results.
Caveat: Editorial qualification: paid placements must use rel="sponsored" or rel="nofollow"; do not buy unqualified links to manipulate rankings. Assess sponsored placements by relevant referral traffic and commercial value, not promised ranking credit.

Tags: search, link-building, digital-pr, content-strategy, brand-authority
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

## Build a keyword strategy combining money pages and backlinks
Use when: Planning a quarter of SEO work for a store: which pages to create/optimize and which links to acquire.
- Do keyword research to identify transactional keywords (category/product intent) rather than informational ones.
- Map top keywords to new or existing collection pages, creating pages where gaps exist (source case: ~40 new collection pages).
- Build 10-20 quality backlinks to those money pages via link insertions and relevant outreach.
- Deprioritize informational blog keywords unless they support a transactional page.
- Review organic search revenue after 3-6 months and reallocate toward pages/keywords showing traction.
- Keep the plan lean: the source argues success comes from focusing on money pages plus backlinks, not from doing everything.
Caveat: The ~40-page example is a single case; the number of pages needed depends on catalog and niche.
Caveat: Timelines in the source's examples (3-11 months) are illustrative, not promises.
Caveat: Editorial qualification: paid placements must use rel="sponsored" or rel="nofollow"; do not buy unqualified links to manipulate rankings. Assess sponsored placements by relevant referral traffic and commercial value, not promised ranking credit.

Tags: search, keyword-research, money-pages, link-building, planning
Source: $40,000,000 Worth of Ecommerce SEO Advice in 11 Minutes.docx

## Technical SEO on Shopify: Audit Order and Purpose
Use when: Before investing in content marketing or link building for your store; when organic rankings are stagnant and you don't know if the technical foundation is broken.
- Understand the mechanism: Google spends a limited crawl budget per site, so cleaning up duplicates and low-value pages lowers the 'cost of retrieval' and improves your odds of ranking.
- Step 1 - Manually review the store for the known Shopify issue classes: product URL duplication from collection paths, auto-generated tag/vendor/type pages, unredirected deleted products, variant duplication, missing structured data.
- Step 2 - Crawl the whole site with a crawler such as Sitebulb, using the Chrome (JS-rendering) crawler and a mobile user agent since Google uses mobile-first indexing. Enable structured data checks in crawl settings.
- Step 3 - Open Google Search Console and review Page Indexing for 404s, 'crawled - currently not indexed,' robots.txt blocks, and the Shopping/Enhancements structured data reports.
- Prioritize critical and high-severity crawl issues first, then work down the list. Fix redirects on unintended 404s (301 to the relevant live page) to recover traffic and preserve link equity.
Caveat: Presenter's opinion: technical SEO is the mandatory first step; skipping it undermines even excellent content and backlinks.
Caveat: Sitebulb and GSC interfaces change - verify current menu names.
Caveat: Large stores with thousands of pages are most affected, but small stores still benefit.

Tags: search, technical-seo, site-audit, search-console, crawlability, indexing, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Fix Shopify Product URL Duplication (Within-Collection Paths)
Use when: When auditing technical SEO on a Shopify store and product URLs appear as /collections/x/products/y when navigated from a collection.
- Navigate to a product via two different collections and confirm the URL changes (e.g., .../collections/healthy-beef-jerky/products/... vs .../collections/jerky/products/...).
- View source and locate the canonical tag: Shopify usually still points canonical to the clean /products/handle URL, so Google needs to reconcile multiple URLs and duplicates waste crawl budget.
- Go to Shopify admin > Online Store > Edit code, open Snippets and find the product grid snippet (commonly main-collection-product-grid.liquid or another file with 'grid' in the name).
- Search the snippet for 'within_collection' and delete that parameter, then save. Repeat for all instances of within_collection across theme files.
- Note that some newer themes already omit this parameter - if you can't find it, no action is needed.
Caveat: Editing this can break URL-derived breadcrumbs - see the breadcrumb meta field card.
Caveat: Always back up your theme before editing; if unsure, ask a developer or AI assistant where to place code.
Caveat: Remove from every occurrence, not just the first.

Tags: search, technical-seo, url-structure, shopify, theme-code, crawlability
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Rebuild Shopify Breadcrumbs With a Meta Field After URL Fix
Use when: After removing the within_collection parameter (or any time breadcrumbs on product/collection pages disappear or show wrong hierarchy).
- In Shopify admin, go to Content > Metaobjects > Manage definitions, select Products, and create a new definition such as 'breadcrumb'.
- Set it to global and choose type URL (avoid the collection type, which can create issues).
- For each product, store the URL of the collection it belongs to in that meta field.
- Modify the theme's breadcrumb section/snippet to render the link from that meta field instead of Shopify's default URL-derived breadcrumb.
- This also gives manual control over what each breadcrumb says rather than relying on URL parsing.
Caveat: Best done with at least some theme code comfort; the source suggests asking ChatGPT to help place the code if non-developer.
Caveat: Best practice: same manual meta-field approach for collection pages.
Caveat: Full how-to code isn't fully given here - verify instructions in current Shopify theme docs.

Tags: search, technical-seo, breadcrumbs, shopify, theme-code, navigation
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## No-Index Auto-Generated Tag, Vendor, Type, and Utility Pages
Use when: When the store has many product tags (sizes, colors, skin types) or blog tags, or when GSC shows many thin/duplicate pages indexed.
- Understand the issue: tags with spaces become ugly URLs, auto-generated tag pages can't have unique titles/H1s/meta descriptions, and sites with thousands of products/posts produce thousands of thin duplicate pages.
- For product tags and blog tags, add the provided noindex, follow code snippet to your theme.liquid file.
- For vendor and product-type pages, add the corresponding noindex, follow snippets to theme.liquid as well.
- Also noindex site utility pages: all collections, all products, new products - they target no keywords and serve no SEO purpose.
- Do NOT index tags you actually want: create a real dedicated collection page for that tag instead (e.g., 'moisturizers for dry skin'), so you control H1, URL, title, and content.
Caveat: Find the correct spot in theme.liquid via a developer or AI assistant - Shopify's theme structure varies by theme.
Caveat: Noindex, follow preserves link equity while preventing indexation.

Tags: search, technical-seo, indexing, shopify, tag-pages, thin-content
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Custom robots.txt on Shopify: Block Low-Value Pages
Use when: After you have set low-value pages to noindex, as defense-in-depth to save crawl budget from duplicate/tag/vendor churn.
- Go to Shopify admin > Online Store > Edit code > Templates and click Add a new template, selecting the robots.txt template type.
- Add rules blocking crawling of: all product collections, vendor pages, product type pages, product tag pages, and blog tag pages.
- Replace any placeholder domain with your real domain in the snippets.
- Check your current robots.txt by visiting yourdomain.com/robots.txt.
- PREREQUISITE: Only block these if you already fixed the product URL duplication, since mismatched rules can create crawl problems for products.
Caveat: Proceed with caution - the presenter explicitly warns that these rules can cause crawl issues if you don't fully understand them.
Caveat: If already noindexed via other means, robots.txt changes add redundancy rather than new treatment.
Caveat: Verify current Shopify template behavior against live docs before deploying.

Tags: search, technical-seo, robots-txt, crawl-budget, shopify, indexing
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Manage Variant Duplication: Merge or Canonicalize
Use when: When color/size variants each create near-duplicate product listings (e.g., cotton sock green vs cotton sock black) with no distinct descriptions or search volume.
- Assess whether the variant difference has meaningful search demand and whether the variant page would have genuinely unique content (description, H1, title).
- Option 1 - Merge: create one Shopify product with the variants as options, keeping one URL and one content set. This keeps the site clean and avoids duplicate pages.
- Option 2 - Canonicalize: create separate variant products but use a custom metafield holding the canonical URL, editable in bulk via Shopify admin (paste the primary product URL for each variant).
- Or update the theme to read that canonical metafield and emit the canonical tag - ask a developer or AI assistant for placement.
- Goal either way: search engines index only the primary product page while users can still browse variants.
Caveat: Do not create separate variant pages when there is no significant search volume - that creates thin duplicates.
Caveat: Presenter's judgment, not a hard benchmark; the threshold of 'significant search volume' is contextual.

Tags: search, technical-seo, product-variants, canonical-tags, duplicate-content, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Redirect Deleted Products to Preserve Redirect Path and Link Equity
Use when: Whenever a product is removed or discontinued and was previously live, especially if it received traffic or backlinks.
- Before or immediately after deleting, go to Shopify admin > Online Store > Navigation > View URL redirects.
- Add a redirect from the deleted product URL to a relevant replacement product or collection page - not the homepage unless nothing relevant exists.
- Save the redirect.
- Confirm in Google Search Console that old URLs return 301s rather than 404s.
- This preserves link equity from any external links to the deleted product and prevents bad user experiences.
Caveat: A 404 does not automatically harm rankings, but redirecting relevant pages preserves equity and UX per the source's practice.
Caveat: Redirect to genuinely relevant pages; redirecting everything to the homepage is noted as suboptimal.

Tags: search, technical-seo, redirects, link-equity, shopify, user-experience
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Remove Pages from Shopify Sitemaps Correctly (Avoid the Hidden SEO Trap)
Use when: When you canonicalize or want to exclude pages from the sitemap without preventing them from ranking (e.g., variant or parameter pages).
- Unlike WordPress, Shopify does NOT automatically remove canonicalized/noindexed pages from the sitemap.
- Shopify exposes a hidden SEO field for products/pages (accessible via a special admin URL path; verify current mechanics). Setting its toggle to true removes the URL from the sitemap.
- CAUTION: setting the hidden SEO toggle also sets the page to noindex, nofollow - bad if you only wanted sitemap exclusion.
- To bypass that side effect, edit theme.liquid, find the relevant section, and replace it with the provided code snippet so the page is excluded from the sitemap without noindex, nofollow.
- Test by requesting the sitemap after the change.
Caveat: Editing theme code without developer experience risks breaking structured output; the presenter advises caution.
Caveat: Shopify admin internals change - validate the current hidden SEO field behavior before relying on it.
Caveat: Sitemaps support discovery; submission does not guarantee crawling, indexing or rankings.

Tags: search, technical-seo, sitemap, shopify, indexing, theme-code
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Implement Product, Article, and Breadcrumb Structured Data
Use when: When you want rich snippets for product and blog listings, or when auditing technical SEO to ensure schema is valid.
- Product schema: most Shopify themes include basic product schema, but verify it is actually output and includes the key properties your theme supports (price, availability, ratings, shipping/return info where relevant).
- Article schema for blog posts: check the theme's default output and verify key properties are present; blog articles pulling in rich results supports content-led SEO.
- BreadcrumbList schema: check the theme's breadcrumbs.liquid snippet under Snippets to ensure it emits BreadcrumbList markup that reflects your site hierarchy.
- Use GSC's Shopping/Enhancements structured data report and a crawler's structured data check to catch errors (often trivial, like a missing comma).
- If implementation is beyond you, get a developer - the payoff is richer SERP presentation and clearer context for Google.
Caveat: Exact key property lists weren't enumerated in the source; verify against schema.org and current Google rich results requirements.
Caveat: Schema issues can silently lose rich snippet eligibility.

Tags: search, technical-seo, structured-data, schema, rich-snippets, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

## Posture Site Speed: Avoid Bloat Rather Than Optimize
Use when: When store pages feel slow, mobile conversion lags, or before adding new apps, sliders, or hero assets.
- Acknowledge the baseline: Shopify's hosting, browser caching, and CDN make a bare store fast. Speed degrades from added factors.
- Diagnose with Google PageSpeed Insights to see actual bottlenecks and third-party impact.
- Apply the fixes in priority order: lightweight theme, image format/size optimization, lazy loading, removing dead app code, conditional app scripts, minification.
- Treat speed as both a conversion lever (bounce/abandonment) and a (modest) ranking signal, per the source's cited data.
Caveat: The source cites Semrush/Search Engine Watch graphics for speed-to-conversion and speed-to-ranking relationships but reports the ranking effect as slight.
Caveat: Precise speed benchmarks vary by store and connection; no universal target is established here.

Tags: search, site-speed, conversion-optimization, shopify, pagespeed, diagnostics
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Choose Lightweight Themes and Cut Hero/Grid Bloat
Use when: At store build time or when auditing an existing Shopify theme that feels sluggish on mobile.
- If starting fresh, choose a lightweight, efficiently coded theme: Dawn is suggested for free, Palo Alto when budget allows.
- Audit an existing theme for bloat: count sliders/carousels, hero styles, and oversized product grids.
- Replace a slider with a single static hero image.
- Cut product carousels from large counts (the source's example: 64 items) to a manageable number (~8) if the design allows.
- These structural simplifications improve speed without needing code-level optimization.
Caveat: Theme recommendations reflect the presenter's opinion and may date; verify current theme marketplace options.
Caveat: Reducing components can affect merchandising/design - balance speed with conversion needs.

Tags: search, site-speed, theme-selection, merchandising, shopify, mobile
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Optimize Image Format and Compression for Speed
Use when: When preparing product photography, hero images, logos, or auditing an image-heavy collection page.
- Choose format by role: WebP or JPEG for product photos; WebP for hero images and text graphics (good quality/size balance); PNG for lossless/absolute clarity; SVG for logos and icons (vector, crisp, tiny).
- Compress existing images app-side: the source uses Crush Pixels (Shopify app) which auto-compresses on upload, commonly cutting file sizes ~50%.
- For manual compression, the source cites Crush Pics app results (nearly identical visual quality, ~50% smaller).
- Resize images before upload so you never upload desktop-resolution images for mobile consumption.
- Adopt srcset for responsive images: let browsers load an appropriately sized source per device - mobile loads small, high-DPI loads large.
Caveat: Percentages cited are the presenter's example, not a guaranteed benchmark.
Caveat: srcset requires theme code edits - may need a developer.
Caveat: App names/features change; verify current Shopify app listings.

Tags: search, site-speed, images, webp, shopify, core-web-vitals
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Lazy Load Offscreen Images on Collection Pages
Use when: On collection/category pages with many product images (e.g., 36-grid) where only the first rows are visible at load.
- Understand the mechanism: by default the browser loads every image at once, but only the first visible rows matter for initial load time.
- Add the lazysizes.js library to the theme's assets folder.
- Modify theme.liquid to include the lazysizes.js script.
- Update image tags: replace the src attribute with data-src and add the lazyload class to the image.
- Save and test on mobile - offscreen images should only load as the user scrolls.
Caveat: Over-lazy-loading above-the-fold images can hurt LCP - do not lazy-load the hero.
Caveat: Source gives one library (lazysizes); modern browsers support native loading='lazy' as an alternative to verify.
Caveat: Requires theme code deployment; back up first.

Tags: search, site-speed, lazy-loading, collections, shopify, theme-code
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Audit and Remove Unused App Code from Themes
Use when: After uninstalling apps, or when third-party script overhead is flagged on the store.
- Recognize that uninstalling an app does not always remove its code injections from the theme.
- Run Google PageSpeed Insights and read the 'Reduce the impact of third-party code' findings.
- Look for scripts serving from domains of apps you no longer use, and any code not serving the current setup.
- Remove obsolete app code from theme files, or have a developer do it to avoid breaking live functionality.
- Only install essential apps; where possible, bake functionality into theme code instead of adding another app.
Caveat: Removing the wrong snippet can break live features - the source recommends a developer for pinpoint-safe removal.
Caveat: There's no built-in Shopify 'app cleanup' guarantee; residue varies by app.

Tags: search, site-speed, third-party-scripts, shopify, app-management, pagespeed
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Conditionally Load App Scripts by Page Type
Use when: When review widgets, zoom scripts, custom-option code, or social share buttons load on every page and slow unrelated pages.
- Identify where each app's scripts currently load (typically theme.liquid sitewide).
- Map scripts to pages: product previews/reviews, image zoom, custom options -> product pages only; social share buttons -> blog posts only.
- In theme.liquid or relevant templates, wrap each script in a Liquid condition so it only renders on the matching template (e.g., only when template contains 'product').
- Save and verify the feature still works on its intended page and no longer loads elsewhere.
- If uncomfortable with code, have a developer implement the conditionals.
Caveat: Conditional includes can break if apps assume their scripts load globally - test each feature.
Caveat: Requires theme edit and periodic re-audit when apps update.

Tags: search, site-speed, third-party-scripts, shopify, theme-code, conditional-loading
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Minify JavaScript and CSS to Reduce File Size
Use when: When PageSpeed or an audit shows unminified JS/CSS asset opportunities on the store.
- Understand minification: remove developer-only characters (spaces, line breaks, comments) without changing functionality.
- For a no-code route, use a Shopify app like File Optimizer to auto-minify assets.
- For manual JS minification, use a tool such as JSCompress.
- For CSS, Shopify offers a built-in route - the source notes you can convert CSS to the .doc.css form (verify current Shopify asset/SCSS handling).
- Apply, then re-run PageSpeed to confirm reduced transfer sizes.
Caveat: Keep an unminified copy for future editing.
Caveat: App names and Shopify's built-in CSS handling evolve - verify current dashboard options.

Tags: search, site-speed, minification, javascript, css, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Speed up a Shopify store by minifying CSS/JS
Use when: When auditing storefront page speed and deciding whether technical asset optimisation is worth the effort before other work.
- Understand the principle: browsers don't need the spacing, line breaks and comments developers add for readability, so removing them shrinks files without changing functionality.
- For a manual route, run JavaScript through a JS compressor and CSS through a CSS minifier.
- Shopify gives a built-in option: name CSS files with the .css.liquid extension and Shopify minifies them automatically — verify the current accepted file extension in Shopify's docs before relying on this.
- For non-developers, an app such as File Optimizer can automate minification of JS and CSS files.
- Recommend getting a developer involved if you are not confident, because broken scripts can break cart or product functionality.
Caveat: The source presents minification as one part of a broader speed playbook; it is not claimed to be sufficient alone.
Caveat: Test on a duplicate theme before pushing live.

Tags: search, site-speed, technical-seo, shopify, core-web-vitals, theme-performance
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Async and defer non-essential scripts
Use when: When third-party scripts (live chat, analytics, tracking pixels) are blocking rendering and hurting storefront speed.
- Classify each script: non-essential scripts are those that don't affect core site functionality — live chat widgets, analytics and tracking tags are the source's examples.
- For asynchronous loading, add the async attribute to the script tag; the script downloads in parallel and executes as soon as possible.
- For deferred loading, add the defer attribute; the script downloads in parallel but executes after the main content has parsed.
- Both approaches let the page render and become interactive faster by moving non-critical script work out of the critical path.
- Have a developer implement this carefully and test checkout, cart and product-page interactions afterwards to confirm nothing breaks.
Caveat: The source says this should be done carefully, preferably by a developer, to avoid negative impact on site functionality.
Caveat: Do not defer scripts that the cart, variant picker or checkout depends on.

Tags: search, site-speed, technical-seo, shopify, javascript, third-party-scripts
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Use resource hints and preload to cut load times
Use when: When optimising storefront load speed for above-the-fold assets, next-page navigation, or third-party domains like analytics and social buttons.
- Add a link element with the appropriate rel attribute and the resource URL, placed in the head of the HTML so it is acted upon early in page load.
- Use preload for resources needed immediately on the current page (for example a hero image or critical font).
- Use prefetch for resources needed for future pages, which makes subsequent navigation snappier.
- Use DNS-prefetch and preconnect for third-party resources you know will be used, such as analytics or social media buttons.
- Verify the exact syntax in current Shopify theme documentation before editing, since theme structure and supported tags can change.
Caveat: The source describes implementation as fairly simple but the actual edits sit in theme code and need testing.
Caveat: Resource hints are one part of site speed, not a fix for heavy images or bloated apps.

Tags: search, site-speed, technical-seo, shopify, resource-hints, theme-performance
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Frame keyword research around customer thought process, not just volume
Use when: When planning which collections, product pages and blog posts to build or optimise for organic search on a Shopify store.
- Start from a seed product term (the source's example is blackout curtains) and ask what a real customer would type, not what you would call the product.
- Generate variations across dimensions: material (linen blackout curtains), room (blackout curtains for bedroom), question form (do blackout curtains keep heat out), and buyer vs explorer phrasing.
- Recognise that each variation can attract a different audience or indicate a different stage of the buying journey.
- Use the research to decide which pages to prioritise, which questions to answer with content, and what competitors are already targeting.
- Expect the output to serve four things: more long-tail visibility, more relevant traffic, better conversion from high-buyer-intent terms, and a sharper content strategy.
Caveat: The source stresses that rankings and search volume alone are not the point; search intent understanding is.
Caveat: No specific volume thresholds are given.

Tags: search, keyword-research, customer-research, search-intent, shopify, seo-strategy
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Build a topical map instead of a flat keyword list
Use when: When a store has scattered SEO pages and you need a single plan for which collections, subcollections and blog posts should exist.
- Understand the goal: outline every topic you need to cover to be recognised as the leading expert in your industry, not just a list of keywords.
- Pull the matching keywords for your seed topic from a tool such as Semrush — the source's example found roughly 21,000 keywords containing 'blackout curtain' — and note their volumes and difficulty.
- Cluster keywords by search intent so you know which group of terms can be served by one page rather than many; the source recommends Keyword Cupid for automatic clustering based on SERP similarity, or you can cluster manually.
- Prioritise by volume, then move the highest-volume clusters into a keyword map that lists keyword, volume, and cluster.
- In a second tab (the topical map), assign each cluster a page type — collection, product or blog post — plus a primary keyword, secondary keywords, URL, volume, current rankings and status.
- Track publication status and, for published pages, whether each needs a content refresh, a quick-win optimisation, or no change.
- Use additional sources such as Wikipedia, Google Trends, competitor sites and Exploding Topics to find topics you missed.
Caveat: The presenter uses this as their own preferred method and says it is 'keyword research on steroids'; it is not the only valid approach.
Caveat: Keyword Cupid and Semrush are the tools the source names; interfaces and pricing change, so verify current features.

Tags: search, topical-authority, keyword-research, topical-map, content-strategy, shopify, semrush
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Decide page type by what ranks in positions 1–3
Use when: When a keyword cluster could plausibly be served by a collection page, a product page or a blog post and you need to choose.
- Search the keyword and inspect what type of pages occupy the top three results.
- If the top three are collection pages (as with 'blackout curtains'), target the keyword with a collection page.
- If the results are informational articles (as with 'blackout curtain alternatives'), target the keyword with a blog post.
- If results are mixed, lean toward whatever occupies positions 1–3, because that is where you want to be and Google is signalling the content type it wants.
- Then apply the buyer's perspective: someone searching a broad category term probably wants options (collection page), while a very specific query like a product name, size, or unusual feature combination suggests a specific product (product page).
- Note that very specific product-page keywords may have little to no search volume, which raises the separate question of whether those pages are worth indexing at all.
Caveat: The source calls deciding page type 'a little tricky'; intent is the deciding factor, not a fixed rule.
Caveat: Google's SERPs shift over time, so re-check before committing a build.

Tags: search, search-intent, page-type, keyword-research, collection-page, product-page, content-strategy
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Prioritise bottom-of-funnel pages first on Shopify
Use when: When deciding what to optimise first across hundreds of existing collection and product pages.
- Rank page priorities in this order: bottom-of-funnel pages (collections and products) first, blog posts later, because bottom-of-funnel pages lead to revenue growth and let you see return on the SEO effort sooner.
- Within those pages, open Google Search Console and sort by number of impressions to find low-hanging fruit — pages already close to the top three where a visibility improvement could convert quickly.
- Look for the small subtopics where search engines already treat you as an authority; these pages tend to receive the most impressions, and optimising them plus closely related pages moves the needle faster than switching to an unrelated collection.
- Pair that with a Keyword Gap analysis: enter your domain plus a few competitors, and review the keywords competitors rank for that you don't.
- If your backlink profile is stronger than the competitor's, the source argues you should be able to outrank them on those gap terms — this is most powerful for well-established brands with strong link profiles.
Caveat: The prioritisation rationale is the presenter's experience, not a measured benchmark.
Caveat: Keyword Gap relies on backlink-profile strength; a weaker-profile store should not assume it can outrank the competitor.

Tags: search, prioritisation, search-console, keyword-gap, collection-page, shopify, competitive-research
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## One topic per page, many secondary keywords per page
Use when: When mapping keywords to URLs so one page can rank for many terms without competing against your own pages.
- Set one primary keyword per page — it goes in the H1, the URL and the title tag.
- Add secondary keywords that are variations of, or closely related to, the primary keyword: singular/plural variants, common misspellings (the source's example is 'block out curtains'), and attribute variants that would still be served by the same page and product set.
- Split topics that would clearly show different products: white blackout curtains and pink blackout curtains should not share a page because they require different product results.
- Cluster manually, or use a tool such as Keyword Cupid, which the source says clusters based on the Google algorithm — if the SERPs for two keywords are similar, it clusters them together, removing the guesswork.
- Remember the rule is one topic per page, not one keyword per page; a single page legitimately ranks for many keywords.
Caveat: Cluster tools are only as good as their SERP data and change over time.
Caveat: The source states this as its own operating rule for Shopify stores, not as a Google-documented requirement.

Tags: search, keyword-clustering, keyword-cannibalization, on-page-seo, search-intent, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Assign URL types: collections for options, products for specifics, homepage for breadth
Use when: When deciding whether a keyword should live on a collection, a product page, or the homepage.
- Assign to collection pages the transactional and option-seeking terms: brand names, features, colours, materials, styles — anything where the searcher wants a variety of choices.
- Assign to product pages only truly specific queries, e.g. a combination like 'aqua blue room darkening curtains' that signals a specific product; size and niche feature searches also fit here.
- Accept that collection pages will usually outconvert product pages for broad terms, because a collection offering many options better matches what the searcher wants.
- Use the homepage for broad, high-level keywords (the source's curtain-shop example is 'buy curtains online'), while individual collections cover narrower categories.
- Check for cannibalisation: make sure the homepage keyword isn't also the target of a collection page (a bicycle store using 'buy bicycles online' on the homepage should not also target it with a collection).
Caveat: The source notes it is debatable whether highly specific product pages with no search volume are worth indexing at all, and that some stores may be better off pushing crawl toward collections.
Caveat: The source argues there is no SEO problem with reusing the same products across multiple collections when it makes sense to users.

Tags: search, page-type, collection-page, product-page, homepage, keyword-cannibalization, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Derive site structure and internal links from the topical map
Use when: When building or restructuring catalogue hierarchy and internal links for a Shopify store.
- Take the topical map and turn it into a hierarchy: homepage at the top, then primary collections (e.g. blackout curtains, sheer curtains, lace curtains, kitchen curtains), then subcollections under a parent.
- Create subcollections by attribute — by room (blackout curtains for bedroom), by material (linen blackout curtains), by colour (white blackout curtains).
- Treat each collection page as dedicated to one specific product category and one specific topic, so pages stay unique from one another.
- Use the mental model that a search query is a question and your page is an answer; the easier you make the question-to-answer match, the higher you are likely to rank.
- Feature subcollections inside their parent collection pages — the source's example is MyProtein's whey protein page linking to dairy-free protein, clear protein drinks and protein blends — which improves navigation and makes the hierarchy easy for search engines to crawl.
- Note that Shopify has no native subcollection feature; you create a new collection and treat it as a subcollection.
Caveat: Crawl-depth guidance ('lower depth is better') comes from studies the source cites generally, not from a specific controlled test.
Caveat: Restructuring URLs on a live store needs redirect planning, which this section does not cover.

Tags: search, site-structure, navigation, collections, internal-linking, shopify, topical-authority
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Spot and fix keyword cannibalisation
Use when: When a collection, product or blog page isn't ranking as expected and you suspect competing pages on the same store.
- Check for overlapping targets: a collection page and a product page both targeting the same keyword (e.g. both targeting 'blackout curtains') is a cannibalisation problem.
- Check content redundancy: blog posts targeting keywords already covered by collection pages.
- Check keyword overuse — spreading the same keyword across many pages on the assumption it improves rankings, which the source says actually confuses search engines.
- Fix by applying one page per topic: give each page one primary keyword and split overlapping pages into distinct, genuinely different topics rather than near-duplicates.
- Keep secondary keywords on each page so you still capture variants without creating new competing pages.
- Also look for near-duplicate collection ideas: pages like 'top rated gardening tools', 'most popular gardening tools' and 'gardening tools on sale' will compete for the same keywords and offer no unique value.
Caveat: The failure mode described (internal competition confusing search engines) is the source's explanation; it is not framed as a documented ranking factor.
Caveat: Merging or deleting pages affects existing traffic — check Search Console data before removing URLs.

Tags: search, keyword-cannibalization, on-page-seo, site-structure, collections, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Expand broad topics into subcollections without creating redundancy
Use when: When deciding whether to break a large collection into subcollections or keep it as one page.
- Create as many genuinely distinct collections and subcollections as the catalogue supports, because each one is an additional search entry point and increases total addressable market.
- Split by meaningful dimensions: for a vacuum collection, the source suggests stick, upright, handheld and robotic vacuums.
- Use a 'shop by' menu structure (by size, type, colour, feature) as a brainstorming device for which collections and subcollections your brand could have.
- Use keyword tools such as Semrush to confirm the splits have search demand: enter the main keyword (e.g. vacuum cleaner) and review the containing keywords with difficulty and monthly volume.
- Reuse products across multiple collections where it makes sense to users — the source says a cordless vacuum and a stick vacuum collection can both legitimately contain the same product without SEO problems.
- Do not create near-duplicate pages (e.g. three 'best/top/sale gardening tools' pages); those compete with each other and add no unique value.
Caveat: The source does not give a numeric threshold for when a category is broad enough to split; it describes the expand-vs-consolidate line as thin and requiring judgement.
Caveat: The 'over 80% of organic revenue from collection pages for stores with 50+ products' figure is the presenter's stated generalisation, not a measured benchmark for your store.

Tags: search, collections, site-structure, keyword-research, product-catalog, shopify, total-addressable-market
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Stick to your core topic and avoid diluting relevance
Use when: When considering a new category, blog theme, or shoulder niche that sits outside your store's existing topic focus.
- Map how close a new topic sits to your core in Google's knowledge graph: blackout curtains, sheer curtains and linen curtains are close together; tablecloths are noticeably further; an unrelated category is furthest.
- Keep publishing inside the topical map where every piece of content reinforces authority on the subjects you have chosen to cover.
- Treat the source's warning as a live risk, not theoretical: the presenter describes a site that dominated a niche, expanded into shoulder niches, ranked briefly for a few months, and then lost rankings — search engines recrawled the site, judged it no longer sticking to its core competencies, and rankings dropped for the main topic too, not just the new one.
- Before expanding, ask whether the new content would confuse both users and search engines about the site's actual purpose.
- If expanding, do it only after the core topical map is maximally covered.
Caveat: This is a practitioner's account of one overexpansion experience, not a controlled study or a promise about any specific site.
Caveat: The 'few months' timeline and the loss of core rankings are specific to that case.

Tags: search, topical-authority, content-strategy, cannibalization-risk, positioning, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Optimise the main menu for fewer clicks to product
Use when: When designing or revising the storefront main navigation to improve usability and crawl efficiency.
- For small catalogues with few product categories, a simple 'Shop' menu may be sufficient — the source's example is an outdoor brand using a single 'shop gear' entry that routes to core collections.
- For large catalogues with many categories (protein, bestsellers, creatine, amino acids, pre-workout), give direct menu access to subcategories like whey, clear, vegan and blended protein so users don't have to click through a parent first.
- For stores with varied collections — by brand, style or goal — use a 'shop by x' navigation directly in the main menu; the source's example offers blinds by type, colour, window type, room and feature.
- Set the goal as minimising the number of clicks needed to reach a desired product for both users and search engines.
- Keep in mind the two stated benefits: better user experience (lower bounce, higher conversion) and SEO benefits (crawl efficiency, link equity distribution, reduced crawl depth).
Caveat: The claim that lower crawl depth typically means better rankings is attributed to studies in general, not to a controlled test by the source.
Caveat: Menu complexity can hurt mobile usability — the source does not address mobile specifics.

Tags: search, navigation, site-structure, user-experience, internal-linking, shopify, crawl-efficiency
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Make the homepage a hub for high-volume collections
Use when: When designing homepage layout and choosing which collections to surface first.
- Identify which collection pages have the highest search volume — those likely reflect what your audience is most interested in.
- Feature them prominently at the top of the homepage using banners or featured sections so they are instantly accessible.
- Aim for as few clicks as possible from homepage to those collections.
- Add engaging content or promotions around those featured collections to draw attention.
- Understand the SEO logic: the homepage usually has the most backlinks and is the most visited page, so linking out to high-volume collections passes backlink equity to pages whose ranking potential you want to improve.
- Study the source's example: a blinds retailer whose homepage essentially mirrors its main menu, with shop-by-type, shop-by-colour, most-popular and shop-by-room sections.
Caveat: The link-equity distribution rationale is standard SEO reasoning presented by the source, not a measured result from a test.
Caveat: Don't overload the homepage to the point of slowing load time.

Tags: search, homepage, internal-linking, merchandising, collections, shopify, site-structure
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Handle faceted navigation correctly on Shopify
Use when: When deciding whether to add filters to a collection page and whether those filters should rank for anything.
- Decide whether faceted navigation is needed at all: a store with a few products likely doesn't need it, while a store with hundreds or thousands of SKUs likely does.
- Understand the default: most Shopify themes use URL parameters for filters, and those parameter URLs are not indexed by search engines, which simplifies the site structure but means filtered pages won't rank for specific keywords.
- If you want to target a specific keyword inside a filter dimension, manually create a dedicated collection page for it.
- Treat that manual creation as a benefit, not a workaround: a dedicated collection lets you add unique content and customise the page, which a parameter-filtered view cannot.
- Choose which filter dimensions to offer based on what helps customers narrow down (size, colour, price; the source's example uses food texture, flavour, special diet and life stage).
Caveat: This describes default Shopify theme behaviour; confirm how your specific theme and apps handle filter URLs before assuming filters won't index.
Caveat: The source frames faceted navigation mainly as a large-catalogue tool.

Tags: search, faceted-navigation, collection-page, technical-seo, shopify, keyword-targeting
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Build product grids for both users and crawlers
Use when: When setting up or redesigning collection page product grids on a Shopify store.
- Do not load product grids with JavaScript — the source says search engines typically struggle to crawl JavaScript content efficiently; use the default Shopify Search & Discovery app to generate grids instead.
- Show roughly 36 to 48 products per page, aiming for as many as possible without compromising site speed, to avoid excessive pagination (the source's example is 21 pages for one collection).
- Use numbered pagination rather than previous/next controls, because users and crawlers cannot skip to a specific page otherwise — the source notes some stores only offer next/previous, which forces multiple clicks to reach page five.
- Add product details into the grid — colour options, a short description, size, pickup or delivery — so customers can decide without clicking through to the product page.
- Treat the added detail as both a UX improvement and extra page content, which the source says adds relevance and usually leads to higher rankings.
- Add faceted navigation for brands with heavy SKU counts (the source's benchmark: roughly 200 SKUs means customers will struggle to find items without filters).
Caveat: The 36–48 figure is the presenter's recommendation, explicitly conditioned on not slowing the site.
Caveat: The SKU/collection relationship ('over 50 products → collections drive 80%+ of organic revenue') is the source's generalisation, not a measured statistic for your store.

Tags: search, product-grids, collection-page, pagination, user-experience, shopify, technical-seo
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Add breadcrumbs, tag-style links and related products to product pages
Use when: When auditing product page template and internal linking on a Shopify store.
- Add breadcrumbs that give a clear path back to broader categories; the source notes most Shopify themes include this by default, so check whether yours does.
- Add tag-style category links that let shoppers explore related collections and products — the source's example is a fishing tackle product page linking to vertical jigs, ice fishing jigs, blade baits, shown near the product description.
- Add a related products section to boost sales and reduce navigation clicks; the source's example is Amazon's related-products row.
- Recognise the combined SEO effect: these patterns distribute link equity more effectively and improve discoverability across the product range.
- Treat this as often underused on Shopify stores hosting thousands of products, where effective linking on product pages meaningfully improves site structure.
Caveat: Shopify theme support varies, so verify whether breadcrumbs and tag-style links are native or need code/apps.
Caveat: The source presents the sales lift from related products as a benefit rather than citing a measured uplift.

Tags: search, internal-linking, product-page, breadcrumbs, related-products, shopify, site-structure
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Optimise the three key on-page elements on collection pages
Use when: When writing or rewriting collection page metadata for a Shopify store.
- Place the target keyword as close to the start of the title tag as possible — if the target is 'vegan protein powder', lead with that phrase.
- Use up to roughly 60 characters in the title tag; if you aren't near 60, add secondary keywords or modifiers like 'buy online' or 'shop', or longer-tail variants of the target keyword.
- Set the H1 to the target keyword itself — 'vegan protein powder' as the H1 if that is the target.
- Keep the URL as short as possible and mirror the target keyword — the source's direction is simply 'vegan protein powder' as the slug.
- Remember the broader principle: one page per topic, one primary keyword per page plus secondary keywords.
Caveat: The ~60 character guidance is presented as a working rule of thumb, not a hard limit enforced by Google.
Caveat: Shopify auto-generates some of these fields, so check for template overrides.

Tags: search, on-page-seo, title-tag, collection-page, shopify, metadata
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Decide what to do with a page: publish, refresh, or quick-win
Use when: When turning a completed keyword/topical map into a prioritised production and optimisation queue.
- Build a keyword map first that lists keywords with their search volumes, then cluster them.
- Carry each cluster into the topical map tab and assign: primary keyword, secondary keywords, page type (collection, product or blog post), URL, main keyword volume, and current ranking.
- Mark whether the page is published or not.
- For published pages, record the decision: needs a content refresh, leave as is, or apply SEO quick wins.
- For unpublished pages, the goal is simply to publish that piece of content.
- Keep the two tabs separate — keyword map for research, topical map for the live plan — so volume prioritisation stays visible while you work.
Caveat: The source describes its own template workflow; the specific columns are the presenter's structure, not a standard.
Caveat: The template link is referenced in the original training material.

Tags: search, topical-map, content-strategy, workflow, keyword-research, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

## Shopify Collection Page Content Stack
Use when: Building or rewriting a Shopify collection/category page template to rank and convert.
- Place a short collection description (2 lines max) below the H1 and above the product grid. It should state what the collection is or why to buy it — mobile users skip large text blocks, so keep it terse.
- Add a detailed collection description below the product grid, 500–1,000 words. Cover what differentiates the collection from other brands, features and benefits, certifications, and usage instructions.
- Insert an FAQ accordion below the detailed description; closed accordions cost no visible space so they don't hurt UX.
- Feature 3 manually selected product reviews at the bottom of the page, choosing reviews that contain the target keyword so they help SEO and conversion.
- Use simple HTML or custom metafields for the featured reviews rather than a heavy review widget, to avoid slowing the page.
Caveat: The source frames collecting at least 80% of SEO revenue through collection pages as its own prior claim — treat it as the presenter's heuristic, not a measured benchmark.
Caveat: Content must improve user experience; AI-stuffed keyword content that doesn't help users won't help rankings.

Tags: search, collection-pages, product-page, seo-content, shopify, internal-linking, conversion-optimization
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Splitting One Shopify Description into Short and Detailed Sections
Use when: A theme only exposes one collection description field but you need both a short top summary and a long bottom description.
- Find the collection template liquid file and locate where `collection.description` is output.
- Replace that output with a split-code snippet so the part of the description typed above the split marker renders in the original position (below the H1, above the grid).
- Add a second snippet below the product grid so anything typed after the split marker renders there as the detailed description.
- Alternative: use a 'read more' link — show two lines below the H1 and reveal the long description on click by pasting the provided theme code and a small JavaScript snippet.
- If implementation is unclear, paste the code into a general AI assistant and ask it to walk through adapting it to your theme.
Caveat: Requires editing theme liquid and HTML directly; back up the theme before editing.
Caveat: Code is for the theme as demonstrated; verify it works with your specific Shopify theme version.

Tags: search, collection-pages, shopify, seo-content, technical-seo, theme-editing
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Why Add Unique Content to Hundreds of Collection Pages
Use when: Deciding whether to invest in content for each collection page when you have many collections.
- Recognize that large retailers (Amazon, Target) typically serve auto-generated pages built from the search term, usually just a product grid, with no dedicated optimized collection page.
- Accept that adding content to 200–500 pages is laborious, and treat that difficulty as the competitive moat — competitors won't do it.
- Apply the short-description / detailed-description / FAQ / featured-reviews template consistently across collections.
- Verify the content is tasteful and user-focused; keyword stuffing or AI content that doesn't improve UX won't deliver.
Caveat: The claim that this outranks competitors is the presenter's opinion, not a measured guarantee.
Caveat: Content quality bar applies — volume alone without UX value won't help.

Tags: search, collection-pages, seo-content, competitive-strategy, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Product Detail Info and Faceted Navigation on Collection Pages
Use when: Optimizing a collection page for user experience and adding unique content, especially with many SKUs.
- Show product details (size, color, pickup/delivery or similar attributes) directly in the collection grid so customers don't need to click into a product to decide.
- For brands with large catalogs (the source uses ~200 SKUs as the threshold), add faceted navigation so customers can filter the product grid and find items.
- Treat the added on-page detail as extra unique content that signals relevance to search engines.
- Combine product details with the standard content sections (short description, detailed description, FAQ, reviews).
Caveat: Faceted navigation typically requires an app or theme configuration; verify the current setup and avoid creating crawlable duplicate URLs.
Caveat: No specific traffic or ranking numbers are promised.

Tags: search, collection-pages, faceted-navigation, shopify, seo-content, ux
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

## FAQ Accordions on Collection and Product Pages
Use when: Adding an FAQ section to collection or product pages to answer pre-purchase questions and earn rich results.
- Source questions by searching your target term in Google and reviewing the People Also Ask section.
- Implement as a closed accordion so the section doesn't dominate the page visually.
- Add a lightweight, accessible FAQ answering genuine pre-purchase questions; do not justify it with promised FAQ rich results.
- Place the accordion after the detailed description (collection pages) or in the FAQ section of the product template.
Caveat: The source notes FAQ-rich-snippet display has been declining on the SERP — implement anyway for UX and potential value.
Caveat: Structured data does not guarantee rich snippet display.
Caveat: Use store FAQs to answer shopper questions. Do not promise Google FAQ rich results or treat FAQ markup as a ranking requirement; verify current search feature support.

## Featured Reviews on Collection Pages
Use when: Improving collection page conversion and adding unique content without a heavy review app.
- Manually select three strong product reviews to feature at the bottom of the collection page.
- Choose reviews that contain your target keywords so the section carries SEO value alongside conversion value.
- Implement with simple HTML in the description or custom metafields rather than a review app or widget that could slow the page.
- Refresh featured reviews periodically to keep them relevant to the collection.
Caveat: The source asserts there are no downsides — verify reviews are genuine and compliant with platform disclosure rules.
Caveat: Review apps vary; using metafields may require theme tweaks.

Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

## Deciding Whether to Rank a Product Page vs a Collection Page
Use when: Allocating SEO effort to either a product page or a collection page for a specific keyword.
- Google the exact search term and observe what type of pages rank — product pages or category/collection pages.
- If results are mostly collection pages (e.g., 'flower seeds'), rank a collection page; you're unlikely to outrank the format Google prefers with a product page.
- If results are mostly product pages (e.g., 'lavender essential oil'), build the product page.
- When results are mixed — which the source calls common — ask what a customer would want: a single specific product or a choice of options; match the format to that intent.
- Cross-check with keyword volume: if the specific product has no search volume, target the broader term with a collection page instead, or noindex the product page.
Caveat: SERP composition changes over time; re-check before committing.
Caveat: The presenter's framing of 'follow what Google prefers' is an operational heuristic, not a rule.

Tags: search, keyword-research, product-page, collection-pages, search-intent
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Evaluating Search Volume Before Investing in Product Page SEO
Use when: Deciding where to spend SEO effort across a large product catalog with many custom-named SKUs.
- Check whether each product's name or specific attributes have search volume.
- If products are made-up names (e.g., fishing lures like 'Hack Attack Flipping Jig') or hyper-specific fabric items ('silk/spandex stretch crepe sheen blue' vs navy), optimize the broader collection pages instead (e.g., 'bass fishing lures', 'eco-friendly fabrics').
- If individual product pages do have volume (e.g., 'rosemary essential oil'), rank them — even lower volume, they attract highly targeted traffic.
- For product pages with no search volume, consider removing them from the index rather than investing resources in ranking them.
Caveat: This is a prioritizing heuristic, not a strict rule — a store's business model and product mix can change the call.
Caveat: Conversion rate figures for product-page SEO traffic (presented as 5–15%) are the source's observed range, not a benchmark guarantee.

Tags: search, keyword-research, product-page, collection-pages, noindex, shopify
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Handling Discontinued and Out-of-Stock Product Pages
Use when: Managing product pages for items that are discontinued or temporarily out of stock.
- Keep the page active rather than deleting it — deletion sends the URL to a 404 and loses any traffic and rankings it held.
- For discontinued products, replace the purchase option with a button linking to related or alternative products (the source references REI's 'shop similar products' pattern).
- For out-of-stock products, keep the page and replace add-to-cart with a 'notify me when available' subscription (the source references Brewmate's pattern).
- Acknowledge that rankings may decline temporarily if competitors have the item in stock — zero purchase conversion signals the page isn't satisfying searchers.
- Restock quickly and implement the above to make recovery of rankings more likely.
- Use 'shop similar products' and notification CTAs to keep users engaged so search engines still see the page as valuable.
Caveat: Ranking loss during stockouts is the source's interpretation, not a documented algorithm rule.
Caveat: Notify-me requires email capture and back-in-stock tooling.

Tags: search, product-page, out-of-stock, inventory, shopify, conversion-optimization
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Product Page Content Template for Shopify SEO
Use when: Rebuilding a product page template for SEO and conversion on Shopify.
- H1 = product name plus target keyword.
- Short description on top: 50–150 words explaining what it is and why to buy; include the product name.
- Features and benefits: bullet points highlighting unique features (most users skim).
- Specifications in a table format — includes technical specs, weight, dimensions, size, material — because tables can earn rich snippets and improve clarity.
- Detailed description below: 500–1,000 words covering differentiators, expanded benefits, ingredients, suggested use; keep it tight since the goal is still conversion.
- FAQ accordion answering pre-purchase questions with lightweight code that auto-adds FAQ structured data.
- Product reviews at the bottom; collect reviews via post-purchase email asking for a review in exchange for a discount.
Caveat: Spec-table rich snippets have been appearing less on the SERP, per the source — keep the table for UX and possible rich results anyway.
Caveat: Word-count ranges (50–150, 500–1,000) are the source's recommended ranges, not a measured rule.
Caveat: Use store FAQs to answer shopper questions. Do not promise Google FAQ rich results or treat FAQ markup as a ranking requirement; verify current search feature support.

Tags: search, product-page, shopify, seo-content, structured-data, conversion-optimization
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Product Page Images, Alt Text, and XML Sitemap
Use when: Optimizing images on product pages to improve conversion and SEO, and ensuring images are indexable.
- Show the product clearly, highlight key features, and demonstrate usage (including lifestyle photos so customers can imagine using the product).
- Avoid generic filenames; name images with the product name plus what's happening in the image.
- Write descriptive alt text describing what's happening in the image.
- Use WebP or JPEG to lower file size without losing quality.
- Optimize image dimensions and compress files; a one-click image optimization app/plugin is suggested for doing this at scale.
- Implement lazy loading so images load as needed rather than all at once.
- Create an XML sitemap that lists all images, not just the primary product image.
Caveat: Compression tools and apps change; verify the current app or plugin interface and compatibility with your theme.
Caveat: Sitemaps support discovery; submission does not guarantee crawling, indexing or rankings.

Tags: search, product-page, images, site-speed, alt-text, shopify, technical-seo
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Product Page Structured Data: Product, Review, FAQ, Breadcrumb
Use when: Adding or verifying structured data on product pages to earn rich snippets.
- Implement Product schema (price range, availability, return policy can show on the SERP).
- Implement Review schema to get star ratings; make sure the review app you use supports review markup out of the box, and double-check that it's active.
- Implement FAQPage schema via a lightweight FAQ accordion.
- Implement Breadcrumb schema alongside on-page breadcrumbs for the SERP breadcrumb display.
- Treat schema as a CTR lever: the source contrasts a listing lacking rich results (Wayfair, one line, less SERP real estate) with one showing star rating, price range, and return policy (Walmart).
Caveat: Most review apps implement review markup, but the source notes you must always verify it's working.
Caveat: Rich snippet display isn't guaranteed even with correct schema.
Caveat: Use store FAQs to answer shopper questions. Do not promise Google FAQ rich results or treat FAQ markup as a ranking requirement; verify current search feature support.

Tags: search, product-page, structured-data, rich-snippets, faq, shopify, ctr
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx

---

## Breadcrumbs, Tag-Style Category Links, and Related Product Modules
Use when: Improving product page navigation, internal linking, and discoverability on Shopify.
- Add breadcrumb links at the top of the product page (e.g., Best Buy pattern) and apply Breadcrumb schema for the SERP breadcrumb display.
- Add tag-style category links where applicable so customers can explore related collections, categories, or products based on how they found the item.
- Add 'frequently bought together' or 'products related to this item' sections recommending similar or complementary products.
- Treat these as both conversion and SEO tools: they increase average order value and improve navigation, and easier navigation helps search engines crawl the site.
Caveat: Keep related-product links genuinely relevant; irrelevant links dilute the signals the modules are meant to create.

Tags: search, product-page, internal-linking, navigation, structured-data, conversion-optimization
Source: Beginners Guide to Shopify SEO - Full Course (4+ Hour Masterclass).docx
