## Match search intent before writing anything
Use when: Product selection and content planning: deciding which keywords get a blog post, which get a landing/service page, and which to skip entirely.
- Classify each keyword by the results Google already shows for it. Searches like 'buy Nike shoes' return ecommerce listings, so that intent is transactional; a query like 'how much does a plumber cost' returns explanatory pages, so that intent is informational. The source calls this out as the reason not every search is equal.
- Point informational keywords at blog posts, where the visitor is researching and can be captured higher in the funnel, then brought back later.
- Point transactional/money keywords at service or landing pages built to make the visitor fill out a form or buy.
- Skip keywords where the top results are other businesses, directory listings, branded names, or job-seeker queries. The source walks through examples like a competitor's company name and an industry mascot and rejects them because the searcher is not a prospective customer.
- For ecommerce: build category and product landing pages for buy-intent queries, and blog content for problem/research queries that feed those pages via internal links.
- Verify current SERP layouts in a keyword tool or by searching, since result composition changes over time.
Caveat: The intent distinction is drawn from what Google currently ranks, not from the words alone, so re-check periodically.
Caveat: Source is a services example (plumbing); ecommerce mapping above is the transferable application, not a demonstrated result.

Tags: search, keyword-research, search-intent, content-strategy, product-page, landing-page
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Filter keywords by difficulty and volume
Use when: Keyword research for blog posts, category pages or service pages where you need a shortlist worth producing.
- Do not ask the AI model to invent keywords. The source says a bare request like 'give me 20 keywords for plumbing' returns a guess with no difficulty or volume data, which is the wrong starting point.
- Use a standalone SEO tool's keyword tool (the source uses SEMrush's keyword magic tool; verify the current interface and pricing) and enter a root keyword to generate variations containing that root.
- Filter to keyword difficulty of 30 or below. The source says low-difficulty terms (its example is difficulty two out of ten) are ones a single blog post can plausibly rank for quickly, and that this has happened for it multiple times.
- Filter to a minimum monthly volume of at least 100. The source rejects keywords with only a couple of searches a month as not worth ranking for.
- Sweep the filtered list manually and discard branded terms, competitor business names and unrelated matches before saving.
- Work toward a list of a few hundred to a couple thousand keywords, then export it to feed the content pipeline.
- Use the tool's questions tab for long-tail 'how much does X cost' style queries, and mine competitor domains for keywords they rank on.
Caveat: Difficulty scoring and search volumes are third-party estimates, not Google-published numbers; treat the 30 difficulty and 100 volume thresholds as the source's heuristics, not validated cutoffs.
Caveat: Aim for a few hundred to a couple thousand keywords rather than an enormous list; the source says you only need hundreds to thousands to be effective.

Tags: search, keyword-research, keyword-difficulty, search-volume, content-strategy, measurement
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Build adjacent top-of-funnel topics to capture future buyers
Use when: Content planning and acquisition: filling out the top of the funnel so prospects who are not yet shopping return to you when they are.
- Identify the audience that will eventually buy your offer, then list what they research before that moment. The source's example is a plumbing company writing about tankless water heaters and signs you need a new water heater, which captures prospects before they are ready to hire anyone.
- Map these adjacent topics to a funnel position: the source treats buy-intent queries as the bottom of a funnel and says you should also have content covering all the traffic above that point to get attention and a foot in the door.
- Produce blog content for the adjacent topics and interlink it to the money pages so authority flows to pages that convert.
- For ecommerce, use this for consideration-stage queries about categories and use cases (e.g. comparisons or buying guides) that link to the product and category pages you actually sell from.
- Use a keyword tool's questions tab to source these adjacent topics when a niche's money keywords are thin.
- Track whether adjacent-topic content later converts visitors to money pages rather than judging it on immediate conversion.
Caveat: The source's example is a local service business; translating this to ecommerce is a suggested application, not a demonstrated outcome.
Caveat: Adjacent content does not convert directly — the source frames it as capturing attention for later, so measure it on assisted paths.

Tags: search, content-strategy, funnel, keyword-research, acquisition, merchandising
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Build keyword clusters so one page ranks for many queries
Use when: Producing a blog post, category page or landing page where you want maximum search coverage from one piece of content.
- Choose one primary/root keyword for the page. The source's example root is 'how to unclog a drain'.
- Assemble a cluster around it: variations such as unclog kitchen sink, unclog bathroom sink, drain won't drain, slow drain sink, home remedy clogged drain, what dissolves hair in drain. The source says pull cluster terms from your exported keyword file or have the model propose them.
- Put the primary keyword in the first 100 words of the page so the page's topic is unambiguous.
- Keep the cluster topically tight — the source's logic is that the cluster should let one page rank for many related searches, so don't bolt on unrelated queries.
- In ecommerce, apply this to category and landing pages: one root commercial keyword plus its close variants from your keyword export, with the root keyword appearing near the top of the page copy.
- Expect a page to rank for many queries you never explicitly targeted, and treat the cluster as a way to maximize that opportunity.
Caveat: The source describes cluster members as secondary and tertiary keywords on the same page; over-stuffing unrelated terms undermines topical focus.
Caveat: The exact number of cluster terms is not established by the source.

Tags: search, keyword-research, on-page-seo, content-strategy, product-page
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Reverse-engineer the top-ranking pages into a template
Use when: Writing or rewriting a page where you want to match what already ranks before adding your own differentiation.
- Identify the top three blog results for the keyword, excluding community and forum results such as Reddit, as the source does.
- Have your AI agent analyze all three and extract measurable structure: average word count, average number of H2 headings, image counts, and the subtopics each post covers.
- Use the averages as the target spec for your draft — the source calls this the winning formula and sets word count, headings and images from the average.
- Make this analysis a mandatory pre-step before writing: the source's prompt forces the model to check the live SERP and steal what works before it drafts.
- Add your own differentiators (voice, examples, original data) on top of the averaged structure rather than copying the winners' content.
- For ecommerce, run the same process against category or buying-guide queries to set the required depth of your PDP or guide before writing copy.
Caveat: The source's premise is that SEO is a black box — nobody outside Google knows the real weighting, so mimicry of top results is a heuristic, not a guaranteed ranking mechanism.
Caveat: Averaging structure tells you what ranks now; it does not prove causation.

Tags: search, competitor-research, content-strategy, on-page-seo, landing-page
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Train the AI on your voice, stories and stats before drafting
Use when: Publishing any AI-drafted store content — product descriptions, buying guides, category or landing pages — where generic copy would hurt dwell time and trust.
- Diagnose the problem first: the source reads a generated paragraph starting 'in today's fast-paced world' and calls it boring AI slop nobody will read, and notes that dry technical niches make this worse.
- Collect references: transcripts of calls or videos, emails you've written, social posts, or passages from writers whose style you want to emulate.
- Create separate reference files for voice, humor, opinions, and stories/business stats (the source maintains voice and humor files and updates them from sample posts).
- Include concrete business facts the model can inject: how many jobs you've done, how long you've been operating, real anecdotes you retell to customers. The source says to store these so the model can reuse them in posts.
- Give the humor file explicit rules rather than adjectives. The source's rule is that if the first 50 words don't land a joke or a self-aware aside, rewrite them, with the tone described as a funny friend explaining something over a drink.
- Re-run optimization passes with an instruction to preserve voice and style, so SEO fixes don't revert the copy to bland text.
- If you have no reference material, lift a passage from a writer whose tone you like and add it explicitly as a humor or tone reference.
Caveat: The source is explicit that its humor rule is its own opinion, not a universal requirement; substitute a voice rule that fits your brand and product category.
Caveat: Reference files must be updated as your writing evolves, otherwise the model drifts back to generic phrasing.

Tags: search, content-strategy, brand-voice, prompt-engineering, product-descriptions, customer-research
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Batch a site build: pages, design reference and SOP file
Use when: Launching or restructuring a store's content architecture when you need index pages and templated landing pages fast without a developer.
- Create one project folder and put a single instruction markdown file at its root. Treat it like training a new hire on SOPs, behavior and formatting rules — the source uses this file to encode design references and build requirements.
- Download and install the coding agent (the source uses a free desktop coding workspace with a Claude Code extension; verify current names and install steps as these change).
- Prompt the agent to build only the structural pages you need: a homepage, a blog index, and a service/landing index. The source keeps this minimal on purpose.
- Attach a real design reference screenshot — the source pulls one from a design inspiration site — because without a reference point the output comes out looking like generic AI output.
- Confirm the build renders as static pages (see the rendering card) and open the local preview link to check it.
- Add pages incrementally rather than requesting a whole sprawling site in one shot.
Caveat: The source compares this to 4–5 hours of manual hosting/DNS/editing setup, which is its own time estimate, not a benchmark.
Caveat: Tool names, install flows and free tiers referenced in the source may have changed — verify the current interface.

Tags: search, technical-seo, site-architecture, static-site, prompt-engineering, landing-page
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Check initial and rendered HTML on ecommerce money pages
Use when: Technical SEO setup at site build time, when deciding how pages are rendered and whether crawlers can index them at all.
- Inspect initial HTML and rendered HTML on the homepage, collections and product pages.
- Prefer accessible product facts and links in initial HTML where practical; Shopify Liquid templates can provide this without rebuilding the storefront.
- For Google, use URL Inspection to check rendered content and blocked resources; Google can execute JavaScript.
- Correct missing product facts, broken links or blocked rendering resources, then retest affected templates.
Caveat: Static generation is not a Google indexing requirement. Rendering support differs across crawlers; assess the actual store and target engine.

Tags: search, technical-seo, static-site, site-architecture, crawling, indexing
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Run a Lighthouse audit and hand the report to the agent to fix
Use when: Technical SEO remediation on a new store or landing page before launch, and whenever page speed or crawlability issues are suspected.
- Open Chrome DevTools (three-dot menu, more tools, developer tools), find the Lighthouse panel behind the double-arrow icon if hidden, and run the page load analysis.
- Read the four scores: SEO, accessibility, best practices, and performance. The source's initial results were SEO 92, best practices 100, accessibility 96, performance 53 — it treats the 53 performance score as a page that loads slow and will be ranked lower.
- Expand every failing audit tab first, including the SEO failures, so the report you copy includes the full detail.
- Copy the entire report and paste it into the coding agent with the target: 100% across performance, accessibility, best practices and SEO, plus generate robots.txt and sitemap.xml.
- Re-run Lighthouse after the changes. The source's second run reached 100/100/100 and 96.
- If any score falls short, paste the new report back with the failing reasons and repeat until resolved — the source describes this as a back-and-forth loop until the issues are fully solved.
- Use this on both blog content and commercial pages, not just the homepage.
Caveat: The source's target of a 100 score every time is its own standard; a single Lighthouse run is a lab estimate and varies by run and environment.
Caveat: This replaces needing to understand render-blocking requests, legacy JavaScript, LCP request discovery and network dependency trees — tasks the source says previously required coding knowledge.

Tags: search, technical-seo, lighthouse, page-speed, core-web-vitals, conversion-optimization
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Generate sitemap and robots.txt so pages can be discovered
Use when: Technical SEO setup or post-launch, especially after adding many blog posts or landing pages.
- Have the agent generate sitemap.xml covering all created pages, and robots.txt specifying what crawlers may fetch.
- Open the sitemap to confirm every page you want indexed is actually listed. The source steps through its sitemap page by page to verify coverage.
- Set robots.txt to allow crawling of the whole site except areas you do not want indexed — the source names admin and dashboard login pages specifically as things to exclude.
- Submit the sitemap to Google so the pages get indexed; the source treats this submission step as what actually puts pages into the index.
- Re-generate the sitemap whenever new pages ship so the inventory stays accurate.
- Apply the same exclusion logic in ecommerce to internal search result pages, filtered URLs and account or cart paths you don't want competing in the index.
Caveat: The source's rule of thumb is to allow everything except login and admin paths; the exact exclusions depend on your platform's URL structure.
Caveat: Verification and submission happen in Google's own console, whose interface changes — verify current steps.
Caveat: Sitemaps support discovery; submission does not guarantee crawling, indexing or rankings.

Tags: search, technical-seo, sitemap, robots-txt, indexing, crawling
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Apply an on-page checklist without destroying the content's voice
Use when: Optimizing blog posts, category pages or landing pages after drafting, before publishing.
- Package the full checklist as a reusable prompt or file and apply it to every page, not selectively. The source maintains 80+ signals and applies them to blog posts and service pages alike.
- Include in the checklist the items the source verifies after running it: 2–3 external links, 3–5 internal links, primary keyword within the first 100 words, 4–8 questions per post, and exactly one H1 heading. The source notes posts commonly carry multiple heading levels (H1, H2, H3).
- Point internal links at your own related pages — the source shows the agent finding relevant posts on the site and linking to them, which passes equity to money pages.
- Generate the meta title and meta description. These are invisible on the page but are the blue title and gray description shown in search results; the source confirms them by inspecting the page's head/meta tags in DevTools.
- Add an explicit instruction to maintain voice, humor and style during the pass. The source warns that optimizing naively turns the copy back into dry text and that a fine balance is required, since content people actually read is what keeps them on the page.
- Verify the checklist output by spot-checking several items on the rendered page rather than trusting the agent's summary.
Caveat: The source's 80+ signal checklist is its own compiled list; the specific counts (2–3 external, 3–5 internal, 4–8 questions, one H1) are its conventions, not Google-published rules.
Caveat: It frames on-page/technical/off-page SEO as work large competitors staff entire teams for; the checklist is a shortcut to parity, not a guarantee of ranking.

Tags: search, on-page-seo, internal-linking, meta-description, content-strategy, landing-page
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Separate content pages from money pages and let them feed each other
Use when: Deciding how much to invest in content versus commercial pages, and how to structure internal linking between them.
- Split pages into two roles. Content pages (blog posts) exist to build topical authority and signal that you are active and trusted; money pages exist to capture buy-intent search and convert the visitor.
- Feed the money pages: interlink blog posts to the commercial pages so authority and link equity flow toward the pages that convert.
- Build the money pages where demand exists, using the service-plus-location (or service-plus-category) pattern, because those searchers are ready to hire or buy.
- Use the source's framing that a rising domain authority lifts all pages, so content investment should be judged partly by its effect on commercial page rankings, not only on its own traffic.
- Keep the two page types visually and structurally distinct in purpose, even if the commercial page reuses the homepage layout as a starting template.
- For ecommerce, this maps to guides and educational content feeding category and product pages; track assisted paths into revenue, not just indexation.
Caveat: The source states the domain-authority lift as its opinion, not a measured effect.
Caveat: It also warns that you will not know a content strategy was wrong for years, because SEO feedback loops are slow — so the mapping between content and money pages should be deliberate from the start.

Tags: search, content-strategy, internal-linking, site-architecture, acquisition, measurement
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Zip services and locations into landing pages — without overbuilding
Use when: Landing page planning for a local or multi-market store that needs pages targeting '<service> <city>' style commercial queries.
- List the services you actually offer on one side and the locations or municipalities you serve on the other. The source's example pairs emergency plumber, drain cleaning, hot water repair and leak detector against Vancouver, Toronto, Montreal and Edmonton.
- Zip them together into pages targeting each combination, since the source notes these queries follow a clear service-plus-city or city-plus-service pattern.
- Source the keywords by sorting a keyword tool by cost per click — the source reasons that advertisers bidding on a term is evidence it is a money keyword, and reads the service-plus-city pattern directly out of the CPC-sorted list.
- Build each landing page from a proven template: the source reuses the homepage design so every city/service page looks the same.
- Do not scale this to thousands. The source says to be prudent — probably not even multiple hundreds — because service page content is inherently similar across pages, and near-duplicate pages can be a negative signal.
- For ecommerce, treat category-plus-subcategory or category-plus-use-case pages as the equivalent money pages, and hold the same line on not mass-producing near-identical pages.
Caveat: The source explicitly says there is no objective cap on how many service pages to build — that number is its judgment, not a metric.
Caveat: The CPC figure it displays in its demo is described by the presenter as exaggerated/bad data; typical CPC in its observation is roughly $3–$20.

Tags: search, landing-page, keyword-research, local-seo, site-architecture, conversion-optimization
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Reuse your best-converting landing page across all money pages
Use when: Landing page and PDP optimization: choosing what layout and copy structure to standardize across commercial pages.
- Treat the site as a staged funnel and isolate each stage: traffic to the page, page to lead or add-to-cart, and lead to closed customer. The source says each stage has to be improved separately for maximum throughput.
- Run paid traffic tests on a handful of landing page variants rather than guessing. The source ran Google Ads against five to ten variations to see which performed best.
- Identify the winner and reuse its structure as the standard for all service pages, so traffic gained through SEO lands on a page known to convert.
- Keep the page visually consistent with your proven template; the source notes reusing the homepage look worked tremendously well for it.
- Translate the same discipline to ecommerce PDPs and category pages: test variant templates and standardize the winner, rather than hand-designing each page differently.
- Note the conversion definition carefully — one of the source's winning landing pages reached a 20% conversion rate (one in five visitors), which it describes as converting into a paying customer, so distinguish this from a lead-form conversion rate when setting targets.
Caveat: The 20% figure is one case-study result from the presenter's own paid-traffic tests, not a benchmark you should assume for your store.
Caveat: The source does not describe the test's traffic volume, duration or statistical significance, so treat a winner as provisional and keep testing.

Tags: search, landing-page, conversion-optimization, experiment-design, offer-design, product-page
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Package the whole workflow as a reusable skill
Use when: Scaling content production after you have proven the manual workflow on at least one page.
- Complete one blog post or landing page manually first so the workflow is validated before automating it.
- Define a skill with an explicit input and output: pull a keyword from the keyword CSV, build the keyword cluster, draft the post, pull images via your configured image API, apply your voice/humor/style files, run the on-page checklist, and reuse the already-optimized technical template.
- State the reuse rule plainly in the skill: the same page structure and technical SEO setup gets reused for every future page, rather than re-solved each time.
- Start a new chat session for the skill to take effect after creating it, per the source's note to refresh.
- Optionally schedule it to run on a cadence — the source mentions automating a daily run and having it skip keywords already used.
- Create a parallel skill for service or landing pages, since the process differs from blog production.
- Verify a few outputs manually before trusting unattended runs, since the skill inherits whatever gaps exist in your references and checklist.
Caveat: Where the source found an error on a scheduled run and had to resend, expect occasional failures in automated runs and check output.
Caveat: Skill syntax and scheduling capabilities depend on the current version of the coding agent — verify the interface.

Tags: search, automation, content-strategy, prompt-engineering, scaling, workflow
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Stage ecommerce content releases around review capacity
Use when: Replenishment and publishing cadence decisions when scaling content or launching many pages at once.
- Set batch size by ability to review product accuracy, originality, intent fit and internal links.
- Publish a reviewed batch of collection pages or supporting articles.
- Check indexing and search performance and resolve shared template problems before scaling.
Caveat: The source's numerical posting ramp is an opinion, not a verified Google rule. A publication spike alone does not establish a penalty; low-value content made to manipulate rankings is the concern.

Tags: search, content-strategy, publishing-cadence, scaling, indexing, automation
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Treat off-page SEO as optional and risky
Use when: Deciding whether to invest in backlink and off-site authority work versus on-site content and technical work.
- Understand the definition: off-page SEO is what the rest of the internet says about your business and how much trust and authority Google assigns you.
- Weigh the source's lived experience rather than a rule: it says off-page work used to perform well around 2018–2020, but that on each algorithm update the people who got hurt were usually the ones doing off-page SEO, and it has seen businesses devastated by it.
- Choose deliberately. The source states plainly that it does not do off-page SEO now and declines to present itself as an expert, so if you pursue it, treat it as a separate discipline requiring its own expertise.
- Prefer reinvesting that effort into content quality and technical health, which the source treats as the more controllable levers for ranking.
- If you do pursue it, treat it as an experiment you can afford to lose rather than a foundation of the acquisition plan.
Caveat: This is the presenter's stated personal choice and admitted non-expertise, not a universal rule against off-page work.
Caveat: The source's claims about past performance and update-related damage are anecdotal and undated in detail.

Tags: search, off-page-seo, risk-management, acquisition, content-strategy
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Rank for search to rank in AI answers
Use when: Deciding whether to fund a separate 'AI SEO' or LLM-visibility workstream versus strengthening core search rankings.
- Apply the source's reasoning: when you ask Google, ChatGPT, Perplexity or Claude a question, the tool first searches an engine, pulls the listings and serves the information back — so ranking well in search should carry over to AI answers.
- Therefore invest first in the fundamentals that get you into those listings: crawlable static pages, a submitted sitemap, solid on-page optimization, and content worth staying on.
- Because AI tools also digest page content, keep the voice, humor and story work in place — the source treats readable, non-generic content as the core driver of both rankings and time on page.
- Prioritize commercial pages for the queries where an AI answer would name a vendor or product, since those answers are built from pages that already rank.
- Treat 'AI SEO' as a label rather than a distinct playbook unless you observe specific retrieval differences in your niche.
Caveat: This is the presenter's asserted equivalence, offered as an opinion ('here's my honest thoughts'), with no measurement behind it.
Caveat: AI answer surfaces and their sourcing behavior change frequently; the reasoning may not hold uniformly over time.

Tags: search, ai-search, content-strategy, search-intent, indexing
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Optimize for content quality as the leading ranking lever
Use when: Prioritizing where to spend effort on a store's search program when you cannot address everything at once.
- Accept the constraint the source states: SEO is a black box with 200-plus rumored ranking signals and nobody outside Google knows the weighting or percentages.
- Reduce it to one operating principle — reverse-engineer what Google wants. Google wants ad revenue; ad revenue needs attention; attention needs content people actually want to consume and return to.
- Act on that by making every page genuinely readable: the source contrasts a generic 'in today's fast-paced world' opening against a rewritten version with personality and concrete detail, and argues the boring version loses the reader immediately.
- Recognize the signals this feeds. The source lists quality and depth plus engagement behaviors such as dwell time, bounce rate, scroll depth and click-through rate as among the known signals.
- Use this as the tiebreaker when choosing between more pages and better pages, or between more keyword coverage and more compelling writing.
- For a store, this means product and category copy that holds attention rather than templated manufacturer text copied across every SKU.
Caveat: The 'content is king' framing is the source's simplification of a genuinely opaque system, presented explicitly as such.
Caveat: The engagement metrics it names are described as rumored/known signals without published weights — do not build reporting around assumed thresholds.

Tags: search, content-strategy, on-page-seo, measurement, product-descriptions
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Compress the whole SEO workflow into four buckets
Use when: Planning the scope of an SEO or organic acquisition program for a store and sequencing the work.
- Separate the work into the source's four buckets: (1) finding winning keywords, (2) on-page SEO — what you write on the page and how it is structured, (3) technical SEO — how the site is built, including load speed and core web vitals, and (4) off-page SEO — outside trust and authority.
- Sequence keyword selection first, since the source insists not every keyword is created equal and content pointed at the wrong terms fails regardless of execution quality.
- Deploy pages quickly using an AI coding agent rather than a manual CMS build: the source frames this as compressing 4–5 hours of hosting, DNS and editing setup into seconds, and says one person with the agent can do the daily work of a division-sized SEO team.
- Keep the on-page and technical tracks both active — the source calls them two sides of the same coin and applies the optimization checklist to both blog posts and service pages, on every page.
- Deprioritize off-page work per the source's own practice, and reinvest that effort into content and technical execution.
- Account for the feedback loop: the source warns a wrong SEO strategy may not reveal itself for two to four years, so track leading indicators rather than waiting for revenue verdicts.
Caveat: The '$500k' and '50,000 clicks/month' figures are the presenter's own past results and are not transferable benchmarks.
Caveat: The claim that one operator with an agent outperforms a 20-person team is the source's assertion, not a measured comparison.

Tags: search, content-strategy, on-page-seo, technical-seo, workflow, scaling
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Stage programmatic page publishing to avoid indexing penalties
Use when: Planning the rollout schedule for a large batch of programmatic landing pages, category pages or blog content on a store site.
- Do not dump hundreds or thousands of pages live at once; the source states this is an easy way to get banned by Google.
- Follow the source's example ramp: a small number of pages on day one, more on day three, more on day five, more on day eight, roughly doubling the volume each interval.
- Keep daily volume reasonable — the source explicitly warns against publishing 'a thousand blog posts a day'.
- The same staged approach applies to service pages, not just blog posts.
- Treat this as a cautious heuristic from the presenter, not a formally tested benchmark; verify current Google guidance before large deployments.
Caveat: These day intervals and counts are the presenter's illustrative example, not a proven optimized schedule.
Caveat: The presenter frames this as playing it safe — it's a risk-avoidance tactic, not a growth guarantee.

Tags: search, programmatic-seo, indexing, content-rollout, risk-management
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Why off-page link building is risky and often backfires
Use when: Deciding whether to budget for a backlink/off-page SEO agency or freelancer for your store.
- Recognize the source's stance: the presenter states he does not do off-page SEO himself, having seen businesses devastated by it, and treats his coverage as theoretical.
- Vet any backlinker by asking for a specific site they worked on plus the start and end dates of their campaign, then check the traffic trend over that window.
- In the source's anecdote, even a claimed top-1% Upwork backlinker's example site showed performance tanking (a downward line) between start and end dates.
- Understand that cheap '100 backlinks for $5' offers typically use private blog networks (PBNs) — link farms with thousands of sites linking to you — which Google detects and which can blacklist or permanently damage your site.
- The presenter's preferred path is to skip off-page entirely and focus on ranking for golden-nugget keywords instead.
Caveat: This is one practitioner's opinion; off-page SEO can work in some cases but carries real downside risk according to the source.
Caveat: Damaged reputation from a bad link profile may be slow or impossible to fully repair.

Tags: search, off-page-seo, backlinks, risk-management, vendor-vetting
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Broken backlink swapping as a legitimate link tactic
Use when: Building legitimate backlinks to a store's product or content pages without paying for spammy link schemes.
- Run a site audit (the source names SEMrush) on a relevant player in your space — e.g., a competitor or an industry hub like Homestars.com.
- Identify their backlinks that now return 404 errors — external links pointing to pages/sites that no longer exist.
- Check whether you have (or can quickly build) a page covering the same material.
- Reach out to the site owner noting the broken link and offering your equivalent page as a replacement.
- Expect it to take considerable time and to frequently get no response.
Caveat: No guarantee the site owner agrees; response rates are low and effort is high.
Caveat: Requires having (or building) a genuinely equivalent page — do not offer an unrelated page.

Tags: search, backlinks, off-page-seo, link-building, competitive-research
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Guest posting for backlinks via 'write for us' searches
Use when: Acquiring referral links and authority to a store or brand site through content contribution.
- Go to Google and search '[your industry] + write for us' (e.g., 'plumbing + write for us').
- Use quotation marks to force exact-match results, as the source instructs.
- Review the sites that appear — these are companies actively soliciting guest blog posts.
- Write a post for them; you get the visibility and, critically, a link back to your site (a backlink).
- Note the source's definition: a backlink is any external site — could be a blog, YouTube, Facebook, Instagram or any online resource — linking to your site.
Caveat: Guest posting requires producing quality content and may have editorial requirements.
Caveat: Search operators and results can change; verify current search behavior.

Tags: search, backlinks, guest-posting, off-page-seo, content-marketing
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Hero journalism (journalist query response) for expert backlinks
Use when: Building authority and backlinks for a niche store or brand with genuine subject-matter expertise.
- Monitor for journalists submitting queries or questions they need expert answers to (the source calls this 'hero journalism').
- When you find a relevant query, respond with a genuinely useful, expert answer — the source's example is an expert plumber explaining how to unclog a toilet.
- If the journalist uses your answer, you receive a backlink to your page.
- Treat this as a positioning play: your expertise is the asset being traded for the link.
Caveat: The source gives this as another legitimate off-page method; it requires real expertise and offers no guarantee of inclusion.
Caveat: The presenter himself does not run off-page campaigns, so this is presented as theory.

Tags: search, backlinks, off-page-seo, pr, authority-building
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Paid quality backlinks from reputable domains
Use when: Considering a paid placement to boost a store's domain authority and rankings — a fallback the source does not consider necessary.
- Identify reputable, high-authority domains that offer paid placements or sponsored posts.
- Pay the fee (the source describes a range from ~$10 to ~$1,000) for a blog post or at least an included link back to your site.
- Understand the rationale: links from trusted domains pass 'link juice' — authority, trust and positive signals Google picks up on, which can improve rankings.
- The presenter's own recommendation is that this is not required to succeed; he believes the best approach is finding golden-nugget keywords and ranking there.
Caveat: Sources like Forbes/TechCrunch being pay-to-play is the presenter's uncertain claim — he says he could be wrong and to verify.
Caveat: Paid links may carry policy risk; paid placement does not guarantee ranking gains.
Caveat: Editorial qualification: paid placements must use rel="sponsored" or rel="nofollow"; do not buy unqualified links to manipulate rankings. Assess sponsored placements by relevant referral traffic and commercial value, not promised ranking credit.

Tags: search, backlinks, paid-placement, off-page-seo, domain-authority
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

## Deploy a Next.js site free with GitHub and Vercel
Use when: Taking a locally-hosted store or landing-page project live so the world can access it, using free hosting.
- Create a new repository on GitHub (the source recommends leaving it private so nobody else has access) and copy the provided setup code.
- In your coding tool, instruct it to upload the whole project to that repository.
- Create a free Vercel account, connect/integrate GitHub, add a new project, and import your repo.
- Ensure the application preset is set to Next.js — the source says this is required for it to work.
- Click deploy; the source says it goes live in about 60 seconds and you can open it from the provided link.
- Add a custom domain via the domain tab — buy directly through Vercel, or buy cheaply externally (source names Namecheap) and import it.
Caveat: The source specifically notes the Next.js preset must be selected.
Caveat: Tool interfaces (Vercel, GitHub) change over time — verify the current flow.

Tags: search, deployment, vercel, github, nextjs, domains
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Set up Google Search Console, verify ownership via HTML tag, and submit your sitemap
Use when: After deploying a store site, to start tracking search performance and get pages indexed.
- Copy your live Vercel site URL (grab the full link address so it validates).
- In Google Search Console, add it as a URL-prefix property and click continue.
- Choose the HTML tag verification method, copy the meta tag, and in your coding tool instruct it to add the tag to the site and redeploy live (to GitHub then Vercel).
- Return to Search Console and verify the site (the source flags that a typo in the tag will break verification).
- In the sitemap section, type your sitemap URL — sitemap.xml — and submit it so every page is tracked.
- Optionally set up Google Analytics next to track visitor behavior.
Caveat: The HTML-tag verification is described as the easiest method but is sensitive to errors like typos.
Caveat: Console and Analytics interfaces change — verify current steps.
Caveat: Sitemaps support discovery; submission does not guarantee crawling, indexing or rankings.

Tags: search, google-search-console, indexing, sitemap, analytics, deployment
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

---

## Request indexing of a new page in about a day
Use when: After publishing a new landing page, product page or blog post, when you want it indexed quickly.
- Enter the newly deployed page's URL into the Search Console search bar.
- Click 'request indexing' for that URL.
- Expect indexing in about a day, versus the weeks or months that would normally be required per the source.
- Respect the limit — the source says you typically get restricted to around 10 requests per day.
- Use this deliberately on high-priority pages rather than trying to force everything.
Caveat: The 'about 10 requests per day' cap is the presenter's stated rough limit — Google's actual current quota may differ.
Caveat: Requesting indexing does not guarantee indexing or ranking.

Tags: search, indexing, google-search-console, deployment, programmatic-seo
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

## Claim a free Google My Business listing as the lowest-hanging local win
Use when: Setting up local search presence for a store or service business that serves a geographic area.
- Create a free Google My Business listing for the business.
- The source's example: searching e.g. 'Toronto plumber' returns Google My Business listings as the prominent links users click.
- These listings drive clicks, website visits and calls — the source claims thousands of clicks per month, though he hedges that he could be wrong.
- Treat it as the first, easiest step after deploying the site, per the presenter's priority ordering.
Caveat: The 'thousands of clicks monthly' claim is the presenter's rough estimate, not a documented benchmark for any specific business.
Caveat: Most valuable for local/geographic businesses.

Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

## Post-launch SEO checklist order for a new store site
Use when: Running a systematic launch sequence after a store site or landing-page set goes live.
- Step 1: Set up a free Google My Business listing (lowest-hanging fruit per the source).
- Step 2: Set up Google Search Console, verify ownership and submit sitemap.xml.
- Step 3: Set up Google Analytics to track visitor behavior.
- Step 4: Test landing pages to find what converts best, identify the winning formula, then apply it across existing pages.
- Throughout, keep publishing volume moderate — do not dump 1,000 pages onto the site at once.
- The source also pairs the post-launch work with SEO keyword research and conversion optimization, implying these run together after deployment.
Caveat: This is the presenter's suggested sequence, not a validated causal ranking of what drives growth.
Caveat: Google Analytics and Search Console setup steps change; verify current interfaces.

Tags: search, launch-checklist, deployment, analytics, conversion-optimization
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

## Test landing pages to find the winning conversion formula, then roll it out
Use when: Optimizing an ecommerce site's landing pages after the site is live and has traffic to test with.
- Set up Google Analytics first to observe visitor behavior.
- Run tests on your landing pages to see what converts best.
- Identify the winning formula — the version or approach producing the best results.
- Apply that winning formula consistently across all existing pages.
- Combine this with staged publishing: don't dump 1,000 pages onto the site immediately as the conversion-learning rollout proceeds.
Caveat: The source gives no specific sample sizes, test durations or statistical thresholds — apply sound experiment design before declaring a winner.
Caveat: Results vary by store; a winning page on one site is not guaranteed to transfer.

Tags: search, conversion-optimization, landing-page, analytics, experiment-design
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

## Reuse a publishing skill across page types (blog and service pages)
Use when: Systematizing content production for a store — applying the same generation/rollout process to service or category pages after validating it on blog posts.
- Build the skill/workflow for blog posts first, with the staged publishing cadence.
- Once working, replicate that same skill for service pages rather than rebuilding from scratch.
- Apply the same moderate-volume publishing discipline to the extended page types.
- This lets one production system cover multiple page categories on the site.
Caveat: The source treats this as a natural extension without detailing differences in structure or intent between blog and service pages.
Caveat: Generic 'reasonable' limits are the presenter's caution, not a precise threshold.

Tags: search, content-production, programmatic-seo, service-pages, workflow-reuse
Source: Claude Code + SEO Masterclass.docx

## Bottom-of-Funnel Keyword Targeting for Content ROI
Use when: Planning your SEO content calendar or deciding which keywords to target for blog posts meant to drive product sales.
- Segment keywords by funnel stage: top-of-funnel terms (e.g., 'running shoes') indicate no brand, style, or function preference and low buying intent; bottom-of-funnel terms (e.g., 'running shoes for men over 50 with sore backs') signal a specific problem and near-term buying decision.
- Shift content investment toward bottom-of-funnel combinations that solve an immediate, specific problem and are closer to purchase.
- Pull out all long-tail keyword and blog opportunities that match this high-intent profile rather than chasing broad traffic volume.
- Accept that top-of-funnel content may bring more raw traffic but has very low conversion potential, so deprioritize it.
Caveat: The source frames this as a universal strategy; in practice top-of-funnel content can still build brand awareness and email list growth, so treat the bottom-funnel-first rule as a situational default rather than an absolute.
Caveat: Conversion potential is a directional judgment, not a measured benchmark.

Tags: search, seo, keyword-research, content-strategy, conversion-optimization, product-page
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Internal Linking Loop Between Articles and Money Pages
Use when: Structuring site architecture and internal links between blog content and product or service pages.
- Link each published bottom-of-funnel article back to your money pages or service pages where the buying decision happens.
- On the money/service pages, link out to your best-performing articles to create a continuous loop.
- Aim for the loop to move a reader from informational content (where trust is built) to the money page, then back into supporting content.
- When configuring an SEO content-generation tool, provide your sitemap so articles automatically link to other pages and money pages.
- Ensure external links point to trusted sources to reinforce content credibility, while internal links prioritize your own conversion pages.
Caveat: The source presents this via an automated tool's sitemap feature; if doing it manually, audit links regularly to avoid orphaned or over-linked pages.

Tags: search, seo, internal-linking, site-architecture, content-strategy, conversion-optimization, product-page
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

## Set Up a Local Project Folder for AI Content Workflows
Use when: Setting up an AI-assisted SEO and content workflow and wanting your assets resident locally rather than external.
- Download a free code editor (the source uses Visual Studio Code) to run Claude Code natively from your computer instead of from the web interface.
- Create a dedicated folder on your desktop (e.g., 'claude-seo-workflow') and open it via File > Open Folder.
- Confirm the editor is installed correctly by opening the Claude Code panel from the apps/extensions area.
- Store brand assets — photos, brand files, and reference documents — inside this folder so Claude can pull from them when building content.
- Let the agent save generated assets back into the same folder so the workspace compounds over time.
- Verify the current install and folder-opening steps against the tool's present interface, as UI labels and menu locations change.
Caveat: Exact menu paths and extension names may differ in current tool versions; verify before following step-by-step.
Caveat: Local folders mean you are responsible for backup and version control of your brand assets.

Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

## Build Reusable Skills to Standardize Content Planning
Use when: Repeatedly producing weekly blog and social content and wanting a repeatable, low-effort planning process.
- Use a one-time setup prompt to have the agent build out distinct skills — for example, a blog skill and a social media skill.
- Once built, trigger work by simply instructing the agent, e.g., 'plan my week's worth of content for blogs,' and let it reference the stored skill.
- Reuse the same skill for social media planning to keep formats and standards consistent across channels.
- Edit the setup prompt to include your full business information before running it, so skills are built with correct brand context.
- Approve each step as the agent builds the skill, and answer its clarifying questions to shape what the skill does.
Caveat: Skills are only as good as the brief embedded in them; vague business info produces generic output.

## Automate Competitive Gap Analysis for Keyword Opportunities
Use when: Starting keyword and topic research and looking for untapped angles competitors are missing or already winning.
- Run a step in the prompt/workflow that analyzes your local competition.
- Identify two outputs: gaps in the market you can target, and opportunities already working well that you can duplicate.
- Generate blog titles each paired with a primary keyword that maps to the identified gaps and opportunities.
- Bias title selection toward bottom-of-funnel, high-search-intent opportunities rather than broad topics.
- Review and approve the list of title-plus-keyword concepts before sending anything to generation.
Caveat: The source describes this qualitatively; it does not provide a scoring or volume validation method, so validate keyword data with a keyword tool before committing.
Caveat: 'Local competition' is the source's framing — apply the same gap logic to your actual niche or geography.

Tags: search, seo, keyword-research, competitive-analysis, content-strategy
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

## Use a Dedicated SEO Writer Tool for On-Page Optimization
Use when: Producing SEO-optimized blog drafts at scale and wanting on-page elements handled without manual editing.
- Configure the SEO tool's knowledge base with your website so generated articles align with your brand and offerings.
- Set formatting/structure options and enable calls to action (the source recommends keeping AI-overview-friendly settings on).
- Rely on the tool to produce H2s/H3s, focus keyword, meta description, and image alt text automatically.
- Have the tool pull in a relevant external YouTube video or your own, and use your images or auto-generated ones.
- Send generated articles to your site as drafts and review/schedule manually rather than publishing blindly.
- Take advantage of built-in internal and external linking to trusted sources and your money pages.
Caveat: The source states Claude alone can write good articles but you must then edit in the back-end SEO; the SEO tool removes that manual step — verify tool-specific feature sets as they change.
Caveat: The source's productivity claims ('replace your entire content team') are the presenter's opinion, not a verified result.

Tags: search, seo, content-generation, on-page-seo, internal-linking, tooling
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx
