## Connect Content Tools via API for a Hands-Off Pipeline
Use when: Building an automated SEO content pipeline and wanting to eliminate copy-paste between tools.
- In the content tool, go to integrations and create an API integration to get the API key.
- Provide that API key to your AI agent so it can send approved generations to the content tool.
- Create a second integration linked to your website/CMS so the content tool can push generated articles directly to your site.
- Decide per integration whether drafts go to your website automatically or land as drafts for human scheduling.
- Keep the agent as the orchestration layer: it creates blog opportunities, sends them via API, and triggers publishing.
Caveat: Integration steps and API menus differ by tool and CMS; verify current interface and permissions before relying on automation.
Caveat: Hands-off publishing risks errors — the source author sends to drafts and schedules manually, which is a safer default.

Tags: search, seo, api-integration, workflow-automation, content-generation, cms
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Repurpose SEO Blog Content into Social Posts via RSS
Use when: Extending an SEO content investment into social media and wanting social content aligned with proven high-intent topics.
- Curate your RSS feed from the blog content built on bottom-of-funnel, high-intent topics.
- Pull the RSS feed and generate social captions and titles for each target platform (Facebook, Instagram, etc.).
- Create a dedicated social media skill so the agent applies consistent formats across channels.
- Approve the generated social content before it is sent for scheduling.
- Direct social posts back to the blog articles and money pages so the loop reinforces both.
- Reuse the work already done rather than commissioning new social research.
Caveat: RSS-driven repurposing depends on your blog publishing cadence; low-frequency blogs yield low social volume.
Caveat: Platform-specific formatting rules change, so review captions for each channel.

Tags: search, seo, content-repurposing, social-media, rss, workflow-automation
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Integrate a Social Scheduling Tool for Automated Publishing
Use when: Automating social distribution of repurposed SEO content across multiple channels.
- Obtain the API key from your social scheduling tool and provide it to the AI agent.
- Define which time slots (e.g., specific hours across the day) you want posts to occupy, and which slots apply to which channels.
- Optionally limit frequency — for example, one slot per day per channel — to avoid over-posting.
- Once content is approved, instruct the agent to place each post into the next available slot or a specified time.
- Confirm posts appear in the tool's calendar as scheduled without manual entry.
Caveat: The source author uses this daily across multiple brands but presents no controlled performance data; treat scheduling cadence as a starting point to test.
Caveat: API capabilities and time-slot features vary by scheduling tool — verify before setup.

Tags: search, seo, social-media, scheduling, api-integration, workflow-automation
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Monitor AI Search Visibility with an LLM Brand Monitor
Use when: Measuring AI search (AEO) presence and brand visibility beyond traditional rank tracking.
- Use an LLM brand-monitoring feature with long-tail keyword searches as inputs.
- Monitor visibility — whether and how you appear — across LLM platforms such as ChatGPT and Perplexity.
- Track sentiment of how your brand is described in those responses.
- Compare cross-platform rankings to see where you show up and where you are absent.
- Treat AI citation/link presence as a traffic lever: content matching search intent better is what the source credits for appearing in AI overviews.
- Use this monitoring as the measurement layer paired with the content workflow.
Caveat: The source ties results to a specific tool (Arvo's LLM brand monitor); the underlying practice applies regardless of vendor, but exact features should be verified.
Caveat: Source examples of companies shown in AI responses are illustrative case anecdotes, not guaranteed outcomes.
Caveat: AI-platform behavior changes rapidly; benchmarks should be refreshed often.

Tags: search, aeo, ai-visibility, brand-monitoring, llm, measurement
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Choose Category Content Structure for AI Overviews
Use when: Configuring article templates and on-page formatting for SEO content meant to surface in AI-driven search results.
- Enable structured formatting options in your content tool that produce clear H2/H3 hierarchy and extractable blocks.
- Include a focus keyword, meta description, and image alt text so the piece is fully machine-readable.
- Include a clear call to action and FAQ-style formatting where the tool offers it (the source notes these settings are 'perfect for AI overviews').
- Add external links to trusted sources plus a relevant embedded video to enrich the page.
- Leave AI-overview-oriented settings on and review the generated structure before publishing.
Caveat: The source asserts formatting improves AI-overview pickup but provides no measured evidence — treat as a reasonable practice to test, not a proven benchmark.
Caveat: Over-structuring can harm readability for human visitors; balance both.

Tags: search, aeo, on-page-seo, content-generation, ai-visibility, content-strategy
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Auto-Generate Branded Infographics for Content
Use when: Producing visual content for social distribution without a dedicated designer.
- Upload your own brand images to use, or let the tool generate visuals.
- Use a templated infographic style from within the scheduling tool and instruct the agent to create one (e.g., 'create a trail-marker infographic').
- Have the agent generate the image and then schedule it to post on your channels.
- If it fits your brand, let the agent send the image prompt to the scheduling tool to create images or video automatically.
- Keep brand assets in your local project folder so generated visuals stay on-brand.
Caveat: Auto-generated visuals can drift off-brand; template and review before publishing.
Caveat: The source describes this as a 'crazy awesome' capability based on personal use — not a guarantee of engagement.

Tags: search, seo, social-media, visual-content, infographics, workflow-automation
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Keep Human Approval Gates in Automated Content Pipelines
Use when: Building or running an end-to-end AI content pipeline and deciding where a human should review.
- Approve the list of blog title concepts with keywords before anything is generated.
- Route approved concepts to the content tool, then have output land as CMS drafts rather than auto-publishing.
- Review and schedule drafts manually, then publish.
- For social, approve generated captions/titles before sending to the scheduler.
- Once approved, let the agent handle placement into time slots to keep the automation benefit.
Caveat: Fully hands-off publishing is possible but riskier; the source author defaults to drafts and manual scheduling.
Caveat: Approval gates reduce speed — set them at the points where errors are costliest (facts, brand claims, links).

Tags: search, seo, workflow-automation, quality-control, content-strategy, publishing
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Layer Multiple AI Tools Rather Than Relying on One
Use when: Designing an AI content stack and deciding whether one tool is enough.
- Use a general AI agent (Claude Code) for orchestration: research, title/keyword generation, and triggering other tools.
- Use a specialized SEO content tool as the 'missing link' for brand-aligned, SEO-structured article generation.
- Use a scheduling tool to handle cross-channel distribution and image creation.
- Chain them via APIs so the agent passes approved concepts downstream and receives confirmed output.
- Match your brand assets and knowledge to each tool so output stays consistent across the chain.
Caveat: More tools mean more integration points and cost/maintenance; the source calls the scheduler inexpensive, but verify current pricing.
Caveat: The claim that piggybacking AI tools is what produces strong results is the presenter's opinion, not an independently validated finding.

Tags: search, seo, tooling, workflow-automation, api-integration, content-strategy
Source: Claude Code Full Content Automation.docx

---

## Answer-engine shift: optimize to be cited, not just clicked
Use when: Deciding overall SEO/content strategy and how to measure success for a store's organic channel as AI Overviews, GPT search and Perplexity reduce click-through to product and category pages.
- Recognize the change: tools are no longer search engines but answer engines (generative engines) that crawl top sources, read them, and summarize the answer before the user ever clicks.
- Accept that this means traffic to informational and commercial pages will likely decline; the source shows HubSpot's blog reportedly dropping ~87% (from ~10M to ~1M daily visitors) as an illustrative example, not a prediction for your store.
- Change the goal from 'be the destination/click' to 'be the cited source': success = being the topical authority an AI engine trusts enough to quote.
- Treat AI SEO, AEO, LLM SEO and GEO as the same discipline — SEO. The source's analogy: you can't do the butterfly stroke without learning to swim; you can't do AI SEO without SEO.
- Build the base first: off-site SEO, strong content, backlinks and on-site SEO. AI engines 'shop' the first page of results for sources, so you still need to rank traditionally to be eligible for citation.
- As an ecommerce application (distinct from the source's local-service example): make category, comparison and buying-guide content rich enough that answer engines cite your store when shoppers ask 'best X' questions.
- Track visibility across both traditional SERPs and AI platforms, and monitor clicks, impressions, CTR and average position in Google Search Console over time.
Caveat: The HubSpot figure is a cited example, not a guaranteed outcome; some sites still buck the trend.
Caveat: Answer engines use different underlying indexes (Google uses Google/Bing variants per the source), so optimize broadly rather than for one engine.

Tags: search, aeo, geo, ai-search, content-strategy, seo-measurement, organic-traffic
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Keep keyword research simple: two buckets only
Use when: Starting keyword research for a store's category and product pages or planning a blog/content engine.
- Set up one SEO data source. The tutorial uses DataForSEO via a Google Sheets add-on (pay-as-you-go credits), but Ahrefs or Semrush work too — pick one and stick with it.
- Define transactional keywords: terms with buying intent where the searcher is ready to purchase or submit a lead — put these on your money/category/product pages.
- Define informational keywords: terms where people want to learn or compare — use these to build topical authority and capture upper-funnel traffic.
- Pull both 'similar keywords' and 'keyword suggestions' for your seed terms (the source requests up to 80 per run and notes similar keywords are stricter, suggestions looser).
- Ignore extra keyword-type definitions; the source argues simplicity performs better, especially when starting.
- Feed the raw keyword data into an LLM with a segmentation prompt (business type, location, goal = leads/topical authority) to sort keywords into money-page targets vs blog targets.
- Use the output to assign each keyword to a page: main service/category page gets the head transactional term; subpages get supporting terms; articles handle informational clusters.
- Remember zero-volume keywords can still matter — if you know your niche and shoppers search that phrase, optimize for it anyway.
Caveat: SEO tool volumes are estimates and not an absolute source of truth; use them as a guide alongside your own niche knowledge.
Caveat: Transactional vs informational is a simplification — the source says more granular definitions exist but don't matter for getting started.

Tags: search, keyword-research, content-strategy, seo-tools, product-page, category-page, topical-authority
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Mine low-difficulty, high-volume 'gold nugget' keywords
Use when: Prioritizing which new product/category or landing pages to build first when you have limited resources and a new or low-authority site.
- In your keyword export, review columns for estimated monthly search volume, keyword difficulty and CPC.
- Use CPC as a cost signal: multiply CPC by estimated monthly clicks to see what that traffic would cost via ads (the source's example: ~$17.32 CPC × 1,000 clicks ≈ $17,320 in ad spend) — a reminder of SEO's leverage when it works.
- Interpret keyword difficulty on a 0–100 scale: closer to 100 = harder to rank, lower = easier; treat these as heuristics, not guarantees.
- Hunt for terms with near-zero difficulty and high volume — the source calls these 'gold nuggets' (example cited: 'home equity agreement', difficulty null, ~14,800 volume).
- Apply common sense: a null difficulty on a term containing a real brand name (e.g., a bank's product) is unlikely to be truly that easy.
- For each nugget that fits your offering, plan a dedicated transactional page plus supporting informational pages/articles.
- When starting out or on a new site, favor lower-difficulty terms even if volume is lower — the source stresses you want the right traffic, not all traffic, and warns against 'shiny object syndrome' on big broad-volume terms.
Caveat: Difficulty and volume are tool estimates and may misrepresent real difficulty (brand terms especially).
Caveat: Only pursue nuggets you can actually fulfill — the source explicitly skips an example because the business didn't offer that service.

Tags: search, keyword-research, seo-tools, landing-page, prioritization, product-page, new-site
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Generate unlimited content ideas with question-mining tools
Use when: Filling out a store's content calendar with buyer-education articles that capture research-stage shoppers and build topical authority.
- Take a seed keyword and run it through a question-discovery tool (the source uses Answer Socrates; alternatives like Answer the Public or 'People Also Ask' work similarly).
- Example: a single keyword ('home equity agreement') returned 558 related questions — treat one question as one potential blog post answered in detail.
- Use the tool's clustering feature to group near-duplicate questions so you don't write redundant posts; the source notes clusters can show a combined estimated monthly search volume (example cited: ~710 for one cluster).
- Download the clustered topics as a CSV and import into a spreadsheet to build your content plan.
- Add columns for the cluster's main keyword, the variation keywords with search volume, and the H1/H2 that answers the question.
- Note the questions that point off-site (e.g., people searching Reddit) — these signal community-reputation opportunities (answering as a subject-matter expert), though the source acknowledges this is slow.
- Link each topic from your master research sheet back to the originating blog/cluster using spreadsheet hyperlinks so you can jump between plans and keywords.
- Pick 3–4 posts per keyword cluster to actually write, rather than trying to answer all 500+ at once.
Caveat: Question volume and cluster search estimates come from third-party tools and are approximations.
Caveat: The source itself doesn't fully understand some niche terms (e.g., 'home equity agreement'), a reminder to validate topics against your actual offering before writing.

Tags: search, content-strategy, keyword-research, customer-research, seo-tools, blogging, topical-authority
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Run lean competitor keyword and backlink analysis in one spreadsheet
Use when: Finding product/category or location pages rivals rank for that you don't, before deciding what to build next.
- List 3–4 known competitors plus your own site in a spreadsheet, color-coding your site for quick reference — keep it simple.
- Pull estimated organic traffic for each URL using your SEO tool's organic-traffic-estimation endpoint (set location/language consistently).
- Pull bulk backlink overviews for each domain to see roughly how competitors earn authority.
- Pull ranked keywords for each domain: the source starts with 80 returned keywords, but if the tool reports the site ranks for more (example: 101), re-run with the higher number to capture all data.
- Store each domain's keyword list in its own tab and hyperlink it from the competitor row so you can click straight through.
- Have a human scan the keyword lists for terms you never considered — the source flags useful finds like 'Detroit mortgage broker', 'mortgage broker refinancing', and location or brand terms.
- Watch for the interesting divergence: more traffic does not always mean more ranked keywords; high-difficulty terms can drive traffic, while many low-value keywords may not. Correlate backlinks and traffic across competitors to infer why they rank.
- Feed the compiled competitor data (exported as per-tab CSVs) into an LLM with a prompt defining the input, desired output and an 'expert SEO' persona to produce prioritized keyword and backlink opportunities.
Caveat: Competitor traffic and backlink figures are estimates and will differ from actual analytics.
Caveat: Stick to one SEO tool — mixing data sources creates inconsistent comparisons, per the source.
Caveat: CSV export only captures the active tab, so export each tab separately.

Tags: search, competitor-analysis, keyword-research, backlinks, seo-tools, prioritization, content-gap
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Use AI to turn messy keyword data into an actionable page plan
Use when: Translating a spreadsheet full of keywords into concrete page targets for a product/category/service site before you start building.
- Assemble your raw keyword data (similar keywords, suggestions, competitor keywords) without pre-cleaning it — the source deliberately pastes messy data.
- Write a prompt that defines: the input (the keyword data), the business context (type, location, primary goal like bookings/leads), and the desired output (segmented keyword plan).
- Give explicit context the more the better — the source tells the model the business is in Michigan, the page is about a specific service, and the goal is leads plus topical authority.
- Expect the model to ask clarifying questions (business type, primary cycle) — answer them to improve output.
- Use a standard model (the source uses GPT-5 'fast' on auto and says you don't need the smartest model for organizing SEO data).
- Review the output: it returns the main page target keyword and an optimized title (e.g., 'home equity loan in Michigan' incorporating location), suggested subpages (e.g., calculators, which the source notes can earn backlinks), and starter blog article ideas.
- Extract the H2s for the main page — the source shows transactional demand clustering around terms like rates and '[X] vs [Y]' comparisons — and build those sections in.
- Ask follow-up questions (e.g., 'what transactional page opportunities exist?') to refine, and repeat the flow for every related keyword.
Caveat: Output quality depends entirely on the quality and specificity of input data and context you supply.
Caveat: LLM suggestions must be human-vetted for accuracy and fit — the model may propose services you don't actually offer.

Tags: search, ai-search, keyword-research, content-strategy, product-page, seo-tools, prompting
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Plan website architecture before building ('measure twice, cut once')
Use when: Launching or restructuring an online store's information architecture so categories, subcategories and products map to search intent cleanly.
- Before opening WordPress, Webflow, Shopify or any builder, decide your core pages: home, about, contact, service/category pages, blog.
- For service businesses, give every service its own page; for ecommerce, build pages for categories, subcategories, sub-subcategories and products.
- Create a top hub page that acts as the umbrella for all sub-offerings and links down to each subpage. (The source's plumber example: 'Plumbing services' hub linking to hot-water install, 24-hour repair, pipe-leak repair.)
- Plan internal linking so everything connects from an SEO and navigation standpoint, avoiding orphan pages (pages nothing links to).
- Draft titles, H1s and URL structures up front — the source says lack of structure costs months of rework later, and also prevents content duplication.
- Keep page templates consistent across subpages; identical layout is fine, only the on-page content must differ to avoid duplicate-content penalties.
- Use a visual sitemap/wireframe and/or a structured Google sheet to map parent pages, subpages and their target keywords before building.
- The source doesn't recommend AI website builders yet here, arguing people don't explain requirements well enough and the result lacks structure.
Caveat: Some argue location/subservice page proliferation wastes crawl budget; the source's experience is it works well across niches, but says plan accordingly.
Caveat: The tutorial's example is a local service business — the ecommerce mapping applies the same main-page/subpage logic to categories and products.

Tags: search, site-architecture, information-architecture, internal-linking, category-page, seo-foundations, ecommerce-structure
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Build hub, subservice and location pages that match search intent
Use when: Creating category or 'service in [location]' landing pages (adaptable to store categories by location or product type) that need to rank for high-intent terms.
- Make the H1 and page title the main offering plus the main location/qualifier — the source calls this non-negotiable (e.g., 'Plumbing in Melbourne', or for a store, 'Running Shoes in Austin').
- Use a consistent page order: CTA and main title first, intro, reviews, benefits, how-it-works, another CTA, FAQ, then subservices. The source says tweak if you like but this order works.
- Give each subservice its own page with URL like /services/subservice (e.g., /services/emergency-plumbing) and title '[Subservice] in [Location]'.
- Interlink subservices from the main hub page and/or a navigation dropdown.
- To rank in sub-locations within a main city, create location pages (/brooklyn) with the same structure, and sub-location + subservice pages (e.g., 'drain cleaning in Brooklyn', 'hot water installation in Brooklyn').
- Map everything in a spreadsheet: main page, subpages, URL structure, target keyword, and intent — so you know what each page must link to and which keyword it owns.
- Keep templates identical across pages; differentiate only the actual content to avoid duplicate-content issues.
Caveat: The source concedes location-page proliferation may consume crawl resources but reports it has always worked well in its experience — test for your niche.
Caveat: This example is service-based; apply the same hub/sub/qualifier logic to ecommerce categories, product types and regional landing pages.

Tags: search, site-architecture, landing-page, category-page, local-seo, internal-linking, search-intent
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## High-quality AI content workflow: negative keyword list, tone-of-voice doc, preliminary-question prompt
Use when: You need to produce blog or category copy for your store at volume without it reading like generic AI content.
- Build a negative keyword list: start from a general list of AI clichés ('in the ever-evolving world of', em dashes, 'delve'), and keep appending phrases the model produces that you dislike. Re-attach this list to every generation.
- Build a tone-of-voice document: collect 1+ pieces of content you wrote yourself and liked (blog post, email, LinkedIn post — engagement doesn't matter, only that it sounds like you). Paste them into a tone-of-voice GPT, read the resulting document, and correct anything you disagree with before saving it as a Google Doc.
- Use the copywriting prompt with the keyword/question inserted in brackets, and attach both the negative keyword list and the tone-of-voice doc.
- Instruct the prompt to ask you preliminary questions before writing. Answer them fully; the source explicitly states better input produces better output. Ask it to research and cite sources.
- Have it produce an outline first, amend the outline, then request the full draft. Keep an FAQ section at the end.
- Before publishing, read it yourself as the subject matter expert, replace fluffy phrasing, and add a first-hand experience or story into the relevant section.
Caveat: Source's position: search engines penalize low-quality content, not AI content; do not blindly copy 100% AI sites that happen to rank.
Caveat: For financial topics ('your money, your life' category), backlinking to high-quality sources is described as non-negotiable.
Caveat: Never publish straight from the draft; editing and adding original experience is required.

Tags: search, ai-content, content-workflow, tone-of-voice, product-page, landing-page, seo-content
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Surviving AI search overviews: unique voice, sourced data, simple language, visuals
Use when: You are writing store content (category pages, buying guides, blog posts) that needs to be picked up by AI search results, not just classic SERPs.
- Write in a distinct voice rather than neutral corporate filler — the source frames uniqueness as a core GEO lever.
- Answer the actual questions your audience asks; feed those questions straight from your keyword/question research into the brief.
- Back statements with specific data instead of vague claims, and link to the original source of that data. The source's example: replace 'drinking 2L of water a day is healthy' with a specific, cited statistic.
- Write at roughly an eighth-grade reading level; the source describes this as making content approachable, not dumbing it down. Cut fluff and get to the point immediately.
- Always produce an initial draft, then polish by removing unnecessary words.
- Add supporting visuals (e.g. diagrams, comparison charts) to make the point easier to grasp.
Caveat: The ~40% average increase in AI-overview visibility comes from a Princeton GEO paper the source cites; treat it as a published-research figure, not a guarantee for your store.
Caveat: No specific benchmarks for your niche were supplied.

Tags: search, geo, ai-search, content-workflow, seo-content, landing-page, product-page
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Add supporting visuals to content with Napkin.ai and ship them as WebP
Use when: A product or blog section is hard to grasp from text alone and would benefit from a diagram before you publish the page.
- Take the passage you want to visualise and paste it into Napkin.ai (new napkin), select all of the text so the tool knows what to reference.
- Generate the visualisation and review the suggested formats (the source got a pros-and-cons comparison for equity home loans). Adjust styling if needed.
- Download the result as PNG, then convert it to WebP before uploading. The source identifies PNGs as a common cause of heavy pages.
- Place the visual under the relevant section of the article.
- Add an FAQ block at the end, which the source has the prompt append automatically.
Caveat: Tool UI and free-tier limits change — verify the current Napkin.ai interface and free plan before relying on it.
Caveat: The source notes you can also generate images via the cluster tool's Stability AI integration with a chosen style preset.

Tags: search, content-workflow, image-optimization, webp, page-speed, blog, product-page
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Two types of links every content page needs: external citation + internal money page
Use when: You're publishing a blog or guide for your store and want it to drive traffic to category or service pages.
- Identify every factual claim in the draft and link out to the original source of that data. The source treats this as mandatory for financial topics and best practice generally.
- Identify the commercial pages on your site the article should support (e.g. a service page or product page for the topic) and add contextual internal links to them from the relevant sections.
- If you're operating a service site that offers the thing the article is about, link the article to that offer page so the article acts as a supporting asset.
- If you have a genuine personal story on the topic, insert it into the article — the source calls this an extra tick in the ranking box.
- When generating at scale, set citations to inline links rather than footnotes and review the tool's internal links before exporting.
Caveat: The source frames internal linking with a metaphor (a blog post without internal links is a room without doors), not a numeric rule.
Caveat: Optional publishing tools may only natively connect to WordPress and Webflow — verify the current integrations.

Tags: search, internal-linking, link-building, content-workflow, seo-content, landing-page
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Pillar-and-satellite content plan for an ecommerce or service store
Use when: You're planning the content calendar or site architecture for a store and want topical authority around a core product or destination.
- Pick the pillar page — a main commercial page (services page, category page, destination page). The source's example is a travel agency's 'traveling to Tulum, how to get there and services' page.
- Brainstorm the smaller questions buyers ask about that topic; these become the supporting articles.
- Ensure every supporting article links back to the pillar page, and the pillar links out to relevant support articles.
- Plan these pages into your sitemap/wireframe before building, using a spreadsheet to track page, keyword, and status.
- Accept that any number of pages is fine as long as each answers a distinct search intent — the source states you can't really have too many pages if each answers intent.
- For an ecommerce store the same structure applies: main pages, subpages, and sub-subpages, each mapped to a buyer question.
Caveat: The source acknowledges an argument that large page counts waste crawl resources, but reports it has worked well across niches in their experience — plan accordingly rather than copying blindly.
Caveat: Cited as source opinion from practitioner experience, not a measured benchmark.

Tags: search, content-strategy, topic-clusters, site-architecture, keyword-research, seo-content
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Scale content production with a cluster tool (Machined AI workflow)
Use when: You need many supporting articles produced quickly for a category, destination or service cluster.
- Sign in and connect your own OpenAI API key if you have one (create it in the OpenAI playground dashboard under API keys, save the secret key immediately since you can't see it again, and make sure your account has credits).
- Open 'clusters' → 'create content' and set the topic (e.g. a subtopic like home equity loans), audience (the more defined the better), language, region, and engine version.
- Start with a limited cluster size (the source uses 10) and let it research search intent and suggest keywords and titles with estimated search volume.
- Review the suggested titles and remove ones that don't fit the deliverable — e.g. skip a 'home loan calculator' post if the tool won't build the calculator for you.
- Assign a content type per post (informational, ultimate guide, list, roundup, comparison) and vary them.
- Use the custom brief field for any first-hand knowledge or specific points you want covered.
- Set the author/tone of voice — you can either pick a preset or build a custom one by pasting your own writing sample for analysis.
- Set the research section: include domains you want cited, add competitors to exclude, optionally point it at a specific site to crawl, and choose inline links over footnotes.
- Choose the model. The source tested both a stronger and a lighter model and reports that as long as you fill in all the prior information, both produce good content; the stronger model costs roughly $0.50 per blog post in their experience and the lighter one is faster.
- Edit the generated posts directly in the platform, review the inline source links and any internal links between cluster posts, then copy, export or publish as a draft to your CMS.
Caveat: API costs and model names change — verify current pricing and model availability before promising output.
Caveat: Native CMS publishing was only available for WordPress and Webflow at recording time; otherwise copy-paste.
Caveat: The source explicitly frames this as producing ten editable drafts in the time one post used to take, not as replacement writing: 'I don't want you just to generate regurgitated AI fluff.'

Tags: search, ai-content, content-workflow, scale, seo-content, keyword-research
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Don't buy 'our content ranks number one' promises — learn the fundamentals instead
Use when: You're evaluating an AI content tool or agency for your store's content production.
- Judge the tool against the fundamentals you already know: does it let you define audience, tone of voice, sources, internal links, and custom briefs?
- If you prefer a different AI content tool, the source says use it — the workflow transfers.
- Ignore absolute ranking promises. The source states plainly that a tool claiming 'our content will rank you number one' is wrong.
- Budget editing time regardless of tool. The generation is the speed gain; due diligence and added value are still your job.
Caveat: This is the presenter's opinion, not a measured claim.
Caveat: Adjacent claim from the source: some 100% AI-generated sites rank well, but they recommend not trying to copy them because it likely won't work for you.

Tags: search, ai-content, tool-selection, content-workflow, seo-content
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

## Local SEO fundamentals: Google Business Profile, citations, locality on site
Use when: You run a local store or service-area business and need a prioritised local SEO plan.
- Google Business Profile: claim and verify (requires a legitimate physical office), then fill in primary and secondary categories, description, opening date, phone, service areas, and business attributes.
- Local citations: get listed in relevant directories as local backlinks, keeping NAP (name, address, phone) identical across all of them.
- Website locality: add the location keyword to every service page, title tag and heading.
- Keep the services and locations you claim identical between your website and your Google Business Profile.
Caveat: The source says claiming a profile is getting harder due to scam attempts; a legitimate business is fine.
Caveat: They note local SEO contains much more than these three items, but present these as the actionable core.

Tags: search, local-seo, google-business-profile, citations, store-locator, service-area
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Optimise the Google Business Profile: categories, images to 100, weekly posts, reviews
Use when: You've claimed your store's GBP and want it to actually drive calls, direction requests and site visits.
- Find primary and secondary categories using a category-finder GPT trained on Google's official category CSV. Add as many relevant categories as Google allows; verification may be pending. Categories differ by country.
- Edit the description (must fit under 750 characters), opening date, phone, chat option, and service area. Add suburb-level service areas, not just the main city — and be careful to pick the correct suburb in the correct country.
- Add attributes from both the business and the crowd (e.g. woman-owned, LGBTQ+ friendly) where accurate.
- Upload a logo and cover photo, then upload fresh images regularly with the goal of 100 images. The source cites a BrightLocal study showing strong correlation between 100+ image profiles and website visits, direction requests and calls, with the effect rising sharply past 100.
- Post at least once a week. Rewrite a blog post into a GBP update with a prompt specifying your tone of voice and under 1,450 characters (to leave leeway under the 1,500 limit), attach an image, and set the CTA button to 'learn more' linking to the blog post to drive site traffic. Also use offer and event post types for sales or events.
- Add services under the services section, including services that don't exist as a GBP category (the source's examples: therapeutic gardening, NDIS support coordination) — and make sure the services listed on GBP also appear on the website.
- Add a direct booking link (form, call, or Calendar link) if you offer one.
- Get customers or a friend to ask your most frequently asked questions in the Q&A section so you can answer them — you asking them yourself is pointless.
- Request reviews using the review link and its QR code. Print the QR code on pamphlets and cards. The source's strongest finding: asking for reviews in person after service beats every automated text-message approach they've tried.
- Respond to both negative and positive reviews.
Caveat: The 100-image figure comes from a BrightLocal study cited by the source; treat it as a heuristic. The source sets the goal at 100 images.
Caveat: Descriptions over ~750 characters won't fit.
Caveat: The source notes Google Business Profile is one of the few assets that keeps generating leads even if your website goes down.

Tags: search, local-seo, google-business-profile, reviews, store-operations, content-workflow
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Running a local citation campaign with NAP consistency
Use when: You're building local trust for a new store or a store that has changed name, address or phone number.
- Use a local-listings GPT that takes your business URL (or business description) and returns relevant free directories — first niche/industry-specific ones, then broader general ones (the source's examples for Australia: True Local, Hotfrog, Start Local, Aussie Web).
- Put the results in a Google Sheet with columns for directory name, URL, and a status dropdown marked 'done' as you complete each, so you don't resubmit.
- On each directory, find 'add your business' (main menu or footer), enter your details, and keep the name, address and phone number byte-identical across all listings.
- Do this quarterly-to-annually rather than monthly — the source says once every six months to a year is probably enough — and keep them updated.
- If you don't want to do it manually or you run many locations, a citation-building service can do it; the source says check the service supports your target country first, and notes costs around $3.20 per site via BrightLocal at the time of the recording.
- Agencies commonly resell this with a markup (the source mentions adding roughly 50% or $10 per site).
Caveat: Pricing and directory availability change — verify current rates and coverage.
Caveat: If a citation service doesn't cover your country, the source suggests a freelancer is acceptable for this task since it is not complicated.
Caveat: The source calls this the only service of its kind they'd personally trust, which is a presenter preference, not a measured comparison.

Tags: search, local-seo, citations, backlinks, outsourcing, store-locator
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Localise on-site content: put the location into titles, H1s and metadata
Use when: You're optimising service or category pages for a specific city or region.
- Take each service page and add the local keyword to the service name: 'therapeutic gardening' becomes 'therapeutic gardening in Melbourne' (the source's example).
- Apply the same change to the title tag — target roughly 45–61 characters — and to the H1/headings, which in the example only said 'therapeutic gardening'.
- Run a website-localisation GPT/analyzer over the site: confirm the services and the target locations, then get a page-by-page review of where local keywords are missing from the homepage, H-tags and titles.
- Fix the flagged gaps, then cross-check that the services and locations you claim on the site match your Google Business Profile.
- Note the source's caution about ambiguous place names: verify the suburb AND country (Vermont exists in both the USA and Victoria, Australia).
Caveat: The source says not to trust the analyzer blindly, but it gives a good starting point.
Caveat: Brand tone statements like 'empowering your dog to success' are called out as not being location-definitive and therefore insufficient on their own.

Tags: search, local-seo, title-tags, on-page, metadata, store-locator
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

## Technical SEO scope plus speed checks with two tools
Use when: You're auditing your store's site and need to decide what to check first and what to escalate to a developer.
- Define scope: the source considers theme changes and anything requiring code to be the web developer's responsibility, not the SEO's.
- Test loading speed with two tools to get an unbiased read — the source recommends GTmetrix and Google PageSpeed Insights (both free tiers are enough for a few sites).
- Read GTmetrix's grade. Below a B means there's a problem to sort out. The source describes their test page loading at 1.9 seconds as good, and anything slower than 3 seconds as costing conversions — 'leaving money on the table'.
- Note that your own site feels fast because browsers cache files locally; first-time visitors have to fetch images and CSS from the host, so judge by the tools, not your own experience.
- Use GTmetrix's report to identify heavy assets — the source's example was a video that could be compressed without losing much quality.
- Use PageSpeed Insights for the mobile vs desktop split, and optimise mobile first since Google sets a mobile-first standard.
- Benchmark against competitors: the source's framing is that competing in a very competitive niche with a technically weak site is like entering Formula 1 in a Prius.
Caveat: The 3-second and B-grade thresholds are the source's heuristics, not measured conversion benchmarks.
Caveat: GTmetrix server location can only be changed on the paid version; the source says it doesn't matter much for an unbiased read.
Caveat: Mobile generally dominates traffic except for genuinely B2B businesses — the source says to optimise mobile first.

Tags: search, technical-seo, page-speed, core-web-vitals, audit, mobile-first
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Prioritize site speed fixes before other SEO work
Use when: Auditing an online store's technical SEO and deciding what to fix first, especially before investing in content or links.
- Run the store's key pages (homepage, top category pages, top PDPs) through GTmetrix and Google PageSpeed Insights. PageSpeed Insights shows mobile and desktop separately — optimize mobile first, since Google indexes mobile-first and most consumer traffic is mobile.
- Treat the source's stated thresholds as heuristics, not laws: GTmetrix flags load times over 3 seconds as problematic, and the presenter targets a Google PageSpeed score of at least 80 on both mobile and desktop. A store loading at 1.9 seconds was judged fine; one at 9.3 seconds was judged critically slow and the top priority fix.
- If GTmetrix and PageSpeed results disagree, run a third free test (Pingdom Tools) to break the tie.
- Read GTmetrix's report of the slowest resources to find culprits — heavy images and videos are the usual suspects on product pages.
- Fix in priority order (source's typical high-priority list): serve static assets behind a CDN, compress and modernize images, convert images to WebP, lazy-load below-the-fold media.
- Re-test after each fix to confirm the change actually improved load time rather than assuming it did.
- You can export the GTmetrix PDF report and feed it to an AI tool to translate technical items into a plain-language fix checklist, or hand the checklist to a developer.
Caveat: Load-time numbers vary by test location and network; a single test is not definitive.
Caveat: The 3-second and 80-score figures are the presenter's practical heuristics, not official Google ranking thresholds.
Caveat: Fixing speed helps conversion as well as rankings, but the source gives no measured conversion lift for these examples.

Tags: search, technical-seo, site-speed, core-web-vitals, product-page, conversion-optimization, measurement
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Convert and compress product images to WebP
Use when: Speeding up a store's category or product pages where oversized images are the identified bottleneck in a speed report.
- Identify the culprit files from your speed report — look for image URLs with large transfer sizes (the source example found a 3.3 MB PNG dragging down the page).
- Save the original image and convert it to WebP format. WebP was developed by Google to keep sites fast; in the source's side-by-side comparison the PNG and WebP looked practically identical while the WebP was dramatically lighter (3.3 MB down to ~1.8 KB in that example).
- Do the conversion with a free online tool like Convertio (choose WebP as the output), or use a plugin/built-in optimizer in your store platform if available — the approach depends on the ecommerce platform/CMS.
- Update the page to reference the WebP file and delete the original heavy file so it is no longer served.
- If your platform does not accept WebP (some builders do not), fall back to compression instead: run the image through a compressor like TinyPNG (free tier around 10 images/day in the source) to cut file size (the source example saved ~70%).
- Confirm the visible image quality is still acceptable before publishing, and re-run the speed test to verify improvement.
Caveat: WebP support depends on the platform; verify your builder serves it correctly.
Caveat: Compression does not always match WebP's savings; both quality and file size should be checked.
Caveat: The 3.3 MB to 1.8 KB example is one image, not a general promise of that ratio.

Tags: search, technical-seo, images, site-speed, product-page, conversion-optimization
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Audit title tags, meta descriptions and H1s with a browser extension
Use when: Reviewing on-page SEO across a store's homepage, category pages and top product pages before content or link work.
- Install a free on-page audit extension (the source uses SEO Wallet) installed in your browser, then open each important page to see its metadata in the sidebar.
- Check the title tag: it should combine what the page offers plus location/brand where relevant (the source's good example: 'mortgage brokers in Michigan, Align Lending').
- Check the meta description: add a clear call to action at the end (e.g., 'Apply now', 'Get your free consultation') and keep it within roughly 155 characters — the source ties this to improved click-through from search results.
- Check the H1: each page should have only one H1. Use the H1 for the page's main search keyword, not a tagline. The source's bad example ('align your dreams for home ownership') is a tagline nobody searches; the fix is the keyword phrase (e.g., 'mortgage experts in Michigan'). Move taglines to a lower heading.
- Check heading order: headings should be sequential (H1 then H2 then H3 then H4). A jump from H2 straight to H4 shows as an error and should be corrected.
- Repeat for every important page, not just the homepage. Each should target its own keyword and pass the same checks.
Caveat: The source calls these '1% improvements' — important but not an overnight ranking fix.
Caveat: The exact extension interface and features can change; verify current behavior before relying on it.
Caveat: Heading-order errors are typically minor; do not let them block higher-impact fixes.

Tags: search, on-page-seo, metadata, title-tags, product-page, category-pages, content-optimization
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Add schema markup to money pages to clarify page content
Use when: Improving how search engines understand a store's homepage, category pages and product pages, especially before or alongside content work.
- Check which schema a page already has using your audit extension's structured-data section, or Google's official Rich Results Test. The source found tools can disagree, so cross-check with the Rich Results Test before concluding schema is missing.
- Identify the right schema types for the page. Rather than memorizing schema.org's catalog, use an AI assistant with web search: paste the page URL and ask which schemas it needs from an SEO perspective. The source's example returned Organization (or a niche type like Bank/CreditUnion), LocalBusiness, and product-level types like FinancialProduct/MortgageLoan.
- Generate the JSON-LD code for the chosen type (e.g., LocalBusiness) and inject it into the page's header. How you do this depends on the CMS — search or ask how for your specific platform.
- Apply schema to money pages — the homepage and pages you did transactional research for (categories, key products) — not to every page like About or Contact.
- Verify the markup with the Rich Results Test after publishing.
Caveat: Schema is not required to rank, but it makes the search engine's job easier; the source reports businesses improving rankings after adding the right schema, without citing specific figures.
Caveat: The more niche the schema type, the better it can describe the page.
Caveat: Injection method is platform-specific and must be looked up for your CMS.

Tags: search, technical-seo, structured-data, schema-markup, rich-results, product-page
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Make the first fold instantly communicate offer and next action
Use when: Optimizing a store homepage, category or landing page hero to raise purchase or lead conversion from existing traffic.
- Imagine a first-time visitor: within the first screen they should see clearly what is offered and what to do next. The source contrasts a vague hero ('empowering your dog to success') against a clear one ('Melbourne's #1 dog trainers. Get started.').
- Write the headline around a concrete keyword phrase describing the offer (e.g., 'Positive dog training for obedience, reactivity and anxiety'), not a slogan. Add a short subline explaining the benefit.
- Add one obvious, visible call to action in the hero (e.g., 'Book a consultation', 'Shop now').
- Reduce multiple competing CTAs: the source cites the paradox of choice — many options cause indecision. Concentrate on one primary conversion action even if you sell several services/categories, using the hero to route to the main one.
- Verify with heatmapping tools (Hotjar, Microsoft Clarity) which the source says show declining engagement as users scroll — the more you force scrolling before clarity, the more visitors you lose.
- For a fast audit, capture a full-page screenshot (e.g., with the GoFullPage extension as PNG) and paste it with the URL into an AI assistant to critique the first fold. The source's AI critique independently flagged the vague hero, missing CTA and need for a clear value proposition.
Caveat: The 'paradox of choice' and clarity arguments are the presenter's opinion and general CRO principle, not a measured result for the cited sites.
Caveat: Conversion rates vary widely by niche; the source declines to give a universal target number (1%, 2%, 3% all appear across niches).
Caveat: AI critiques of screenshots are suggestions, not validated winners — test changes before trusting them.

Tags: search, conversion-optimization, landing-page, hero-copy, call-to-action, customer-research
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Win backlinks via journalist-source matching (Featured) as free outreach
Use when: Building free, relevant backlinks to a store's category or content pages when budget is limited but time is available.
- Create a free account on Featured. The free tier allowed roughly three answers per month in the source; paid tiers allow more.
- Open the questions tab, clear all categories, and scan publisher requests for ones matching your expertise and store niche.
- Save promising questions to answer later, then return to your saved list.
- Before answering, vet the publisher's domain authority with a free tool (e.g., Ahrefs' free website authority checker). The source's example, MarketingSherpa, had authority ~83 and was topically relevant to the presenter.
- Write a detailed answer drawing on your own first-hand experience — the source stresses that personal experience is what sets you apart from other respondents.
- Submit; expect the answer to sit 'in review' until the publisher reads and (if they like it) publishes it, which is when you get the backlink.
- Set a recurring monthly reminder to spend your free answers, since publisher requests cycle constantly.
Caveat: You only get a backlink if the publisher chooses to publish your answer — not guaranteed.
Caveat: Domain authority tools are not absolute sources of truth; treat them as rough signals.
Caveat: Relevance to your niche matters more than raw authority.

Tags: search, link-building, backlinks, outreach, digital-pr, content-optimization
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Judge backlink quality: relevance over quantity and natural link growth
Use when: Deciding whether to buy, accept or build a backlink, and vetting any link-building vendor or prospect.
- Weight relevance heavily: a link should come from a site in your industry, locality or niche. A dog-training site should not chase links from computer-gaming gear sites, since contextual relevance is what carries value.
- Reject quantity-based pitches. If someone offers '1,000 backlinks for $100/$1,000', the source's advice is to walk away — bad-quality links can associate your site with bad neighborhoods.
- Vet any prospect with an SEO tool (Ahrefs, Semrush, Moz). Look at the referring-domains trend versus organic traffic. A gradual, natural climb over years is a good sign (the source's example grew steadily).
- Flag sudden, aggressive spikes in referring domains as suspicious. The source allows that a viral article can cause a legitimate spike, but says to find the responsible link before trusting it.
- Treat cheap link packages as a trigger for due diligence, not automatic disqualification — the source says cheap is not always bad but warrants scrutiny.
- For competitive niches, compare your backlink profile to competitors in your initial research; if they have dramatically more links, a backlink strategy is likely necessary.
Caveat: Backlink effects take roughly 3–6 months to show, unlike paid search, making ROI hard to attribute.
Caveat: The source notes agencies often just resell bought links, so outsourcing does not guarantee quality.
Caveat: The source is affiliated with/paid by one vendor (Get Me Links) and says so; treat vendor recommendations accordingly.

Tags: search, link-building, backlinks, vendor-evaluation, competitive-research, measurement
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Buy backlinks from vetted vendors when competitors outrank you
Use when: Considering paid link building for a store in a competitive niche where competitors clearly dominate backlinks.
- Confirm the need first: in your competitor research, if rivals have roughly 10x your backlinks alongside much more traffic and leads, paid link building may be justified (the source's framing).
- Choose reputable vendors rather than cheap packages. The source names Get Me Links (a vendor it is affiliated with) and The Hoth as two it has used, disclosing its affiliation with Get Me Links.
- Use the vendor's strategy call/consultation to get recommendations for your site before committing; the source says these calls are risk-free and educational.
- Pick packages segmented by your industry — the source notes vendors split offerings by industry precisely because industry relevance matters.
- Track each delivered link by anchor text, target URL and the linking site, so you can judge relevance and monitor the profile over time.
- Expect a long lag: the source says backlink ROI takes roughly 3–6 months, unlike paid channels that show results immediately.
Caveat: The presenter is an affiliate of Get Me Links and says so — recommendation is not neutral.
Caveat: Paid links are not cheap, and results are slow and hard to attribute.
Caveat: Treat vendor guarantees skeptically; the source emphasizes reputable sourcing rather than guaranteed rankings.
Caveat: Editorial qualification: paid placements must use rel="sponsored" or rel="nofollow"; do not buy unqualified links to manipulate rankings. Assess sponsored placements by relevant referral traffic and commercial value, not promised ranking credit.

Tags: search, link-building, backlinks, vendor-evaluation, acquisition, measurement
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Separate Search Console (how Google sees you) from Analytics (what visitors do)
Use when: Setting up or interpreting search and site performance data for an online store to decide next actions.
- Understand the split: Google Search Console shows what your site ranks for, average position, click-through rate from search results, and indexing problems — 'how Google sees my site'. Google Analytics shows where visitors come from (not only Google) and what they do after landing — 'what users do on the site'.
- In Search Console, watch three query categories: your brand name (you should rank for it; if not, that is a problem), product/service keywords, and informational keywords you target with content.
- Use the Pages report to diagnose pages that are not indexed — the source notes crawling does not guarantee indexing, and non-indexed pages can explain low traffic.
- Use Analytics to compare traffic sources and, crucially, quality/conversion, not just volume. Inspect referral sources and investigate any that send high-quality engaged traffic.
- Export Search Console or Analytics data (CSV/Sheets) and feed it into an AI analyst tool or a dashboard (Looker Studio template) if you cannot interpret it manually.
- Act on only the numbers you can act on — the source repeatedly warns against analysis paralysis and ignoring vanity metrics.
Caveat: Average position in Search Console is approximate; personalized location can shift actual results, so it is not exact.
Caveat: Analytics needs correct tracking; 'unassigned' sources indicate missing attribution.
Caveat: Set up of both tools assumes property configuration, which is outside this card.

Tags: search, analytics, search-console, measurement, data-analysis, reporting
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Mine positions 9–30 in Search Console for low-hanging SEO wins
Use when: Planning the next month's SEO work on a store's existing pages when you are unsure where to start.
- In Google Search Console, open the Pages report, sort by average position, and increase rows shown (the source uses ~50 rows).
- Filter to pages sitting roughly in positions 9–30. The source calls positions ~9–10 'low-hanging fruit' because they are already indexed and getting some traffic, just not ranking well (page 2 of results).
- For each such page, choose a remedy: improve the page content/title, build supporting content that links to it, or point backlinks directly at it.
- Prioritize these over brand-new pages, because you start from an already-indexed, already-crawling asset rather than zero.
- Use an AI analyst (the source's custom GPT over Search Console data) to surface these opportunities automatically — the presenter notes the tool independently flagged positions 11–30 and suggested CTR and featured-snippet improvements.
- Build a recurring monthly review so this becomes a repeatable process rather than a one-off.
Caveat: The 9–30 band is the source's practical heuristic, not a fixed rule.
Caveat: Improvements still take time and are not guaranteed to move the page.
Caveat: Position bands can overlap with what the presenter also labels as '11 to 30' opportunities; treat them as the same broad bucket.

Tags: search, search-console, keyword-optimization, content-optimization, measurement, prioritization
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Use Analytics traffic-source data to find partner and acquisition opportunities
Use when: Deciding where to invest acquisition or partnership effort for an online store by reviewing real source performance.
- In Google Analytics, go to Reports → Acquisition → Traffic acquisition for the last 28 days to see channels (direct, organic search, organic social, paid, referral, etc.).
- Drill into Source/Medium for more detail on individual referrers.
- Compare not just traffic volume but quality: look at engagement/bounce, time on site and especially conversions per source. The source stresses that a source with less traffic can produce more conversions than a high-volume one.
- Investigate strong referral sources. In the source example, a PC-part site sent a lot of good-quality traffic (about 45 seconds average session) to a PC retailer — the recommendation was to reach out to that site for a closer partnership.
- Check the tech/device breakdown (roughly quarterly) to spot imbalance — e.g., if desktop dominates traffic but mobile converts better (or vice versa), investigate the underperforming device experience and fix it.
- Use the insight to allocate next month's effort toward channels/sources already proven to convert, rather than diversifying blindly.
Caveat: Traffic volume is not quality; a big source can still fail to convert, so always pair volume with conversion data.
Caveat: Attribution can be imperfect ('unassigned'), so treat source labels cautiously.
Caveat: The partner-outreach example is illustrative, not a guaranteed outcome.

Tags: search, analytics, acquisition, measurement, conversion-optimization, partnerships
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Ignore domain authority as a vanity metric; judge by keywords and conversions
Use when: Choosing which SEO KPIs to report or optimize when evaluating a store's website performance.
- Recognize domain authority is a proprietary score from tools like Moz (and similar metrics from Semrush, DataForSEO), each calculated differently — it is not an official Google metric and not a reliable proxy for results.
- Note the source's point that high-domain-authority sites do not necessarily convert, so the number alone is misleading as a success measure.
- Track instead: the number of keywords the site actually ranks for and, ultimately, conversions (form submits, orders) — the source calls this the real aim.
- When reporting SEO progress to stakeholders, frame improvements in traffic and conversions rather than ranking jumps alone; the presenter notes agencies (including himself) historically over-celebrated ranking moves that did not affect the bottom line.
- Do not treat domain authority as useless — use it at most as a rough comparative signal in link prospecting, not as a performance target.
Caveat: Source explicitly calls out its own former over-focus on rankings as a mistake, framing this as an opinion and best-practice shift.
Caveat: Rankings can still be a useful leading indicator even if not the final goal.

Tags: search, measurement, reporting, analytics, key-metrics, conversion-optimization
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Turn raw SEO data into actions with an AI analyst
Use when: Monthly SEO review for an online store when you have the data but are unsure what to do with it.
- In Search Console (or Analytics), select the last 28 days and export the report (CSV or Google Sheet).
- Load the exported data into an AI analyst tool set up for Search Console/Analytics data.
- Ask it specifically for low-hanging opportunities (e.g., pages in positions 11–30), CTR improvements, featured-snippet opportunities, and hidden gems — the source's tool produced exactly this structure.
- Use the output to build a prioritized to-do list rather than doing exploratory analysis yourself.
- Look for seasonal patterns the data reveals — the source notes a PC retailer whose traffic rose with each new GPU launch — and plan content or promotions around recurring demand peaks; likewise, seasonal businesses (e.g., landscaping in winter) can see slow periods in the data.
- Refresh this monthly as a standing habit instead of ad-hoc digging.
Caveat: AI recommendations must be sanity-checked and tested, not applied blindly.
Caveat: Data quality depends on correct tracking and indexing.
Caveat: The source used a specific custom GPT tool; functionality of AI tools changes, so verify the current setup.

Tags: search, analytics, automation, content-optimization, measurement, worksheet-tools
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Beware automating SEO before fundamentals work
Use when: Deciding whether to invest in SEO automation tooling for an online store.
- Before automating, confirm fundamentals are sound: site speed, indexing, on-page metadata, working schema and a converting first fold for the key pages.
- Understand the source's warning: 'just because everything can be automated doesn't mean it should be' — scaling without fundamentals produces worse outcomes.
- If fundamentals are ready, choose an automation platform. The source names three it has used, with Pabbly called the easiest plug-and-play for no-code automation.
- Map a small number of high-value, repetitive SEO tasks to automate first (e.g., report pulling, content brief assembly) rather than everything at once.
- Test each automation on a small scope before letting it run broadly, to catch errors before they scale.
- Re-check the tools' current interfaces and capabilities before building, since automation platforms change frequently.
Caveat: Source's tool preferences and 'easiest' ranking are personal experience, not independent benchmarks.
Caveat: Over-automation is explicitly flagged as a failure mode in real cases the presenter observed.

Tags: search, automation, workflow-tools, process, technical-seo, prioritization
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Add a CTA to meta descriptions and differentiate from competitors in the SERP
Use when: Ecommerce SEO where pages rank but get few clicks, or while waiting for ranking gains and wanting more clicks from current positions.
- Draft a meta description containing a clear call to action at the end (e.g., 'Apply now', 'Get your free consultation') and keep it within about 155 characters.
- Inspect the current search results page for your target keyword; deliberately write a description that differs from competitors' snippets so yours stands out, which the source says can sharply raise click-through.
- Allow a natural, human tone rather than keyword stuffing — the source prefers descriptions that read naturally.
- Pair the description change with an SERP title/tag optimization, then measure CTR in Search Console before and after.
- Layer further testing: the source suggests tools like Microsoft Clarity (behavior) and VWO (A/B testing) to test one change at a time, such as CTA wording — e.g., 'Submit your free form' vs 'Call now'.
- Test only one element per experiment so you can attribute the effect.
Caveat: The click-through improvement claim comes from the presenter's own experience, without a stated measured benchmark.
Caveat: Whether Google displays your exact meta description is not guaranteed; it may rewrite it.
Caveat: Character limits are guidance, not strictly enforced.

Tags: search, on-page-seo, metadata, conversion-optimization, experiment-design, click-through-rate
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

## Automate Instagram media to Google Business Profile
Use when: You manage a local store with an Instagram business account and a Google Business Profile, and want to keep local listings fresh without manually re-uploading images or videos.
- Confirm you have admin access to the Google Business Profile and an Instagram business account linked to the same brand — these are non-negotiable prerequisites.
- In Pabbly (or Make/Zapier), create a folder named after the client/store, then create a workflow named e.g. 'Instagram to Google Business'.
- Set the trigger app to Instagram, trigger event 'new media updated to the account', and authenticate the Instagram business connection.
- Add an action: Google Business Profile > upload media/photo (choose image or video to match the source). Authenticate the Google account and select the target location.
- For the media URL field, map the 'media URL' attribute pulled from the Instagram post in the trigger test data. Optionally set a category.
- Save and send a test. Verify on the Google Business Profile photos/videos tab that the asset appears, then toggle the workflow ON for autopilot.
Caveat: Automation tool interfaces and field names change — verify the current UI before building.
Caveat: Pabbly free tier may be sufficient for a single store; scaling many locations likely needs a paid plan.

Tags: search, local-seo, automation, pabbly, google-business-profile, instagram, merchandising
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Auto-convert blog posts into Google Business posts via RSS
Use when: You publish blog content and want an automated, ongoing stream of Google Business posts driving traffic back to those articles without manual rewriting.
- Create a workflow triggered by an RSS feed ('new item in feed'). For most WordPress sites the feed URL is yoursite.com/feed — for other platforms, look up or generate a feed.
- Add an Open Router 'chat completion' action and connect it using your API key.
- Select a model with native internet access (e.g. Perplexity Sonar) so it can read the post URL directly instead of needing a separate scraper.
- Set role to 'user' and paste a prompt instructing the model to visit the blog URL, read it, and rewrite it in the same tone of voice but under ~1,450–1,500 characters to fit Google Business limits. Map the RSS feed's URL field into the prompt.
- Ask for a call-to-action line and, optionally, suppress hashtags if you don't want them.
- Add a Google Business Profile action: create an action post. Map the message content to the model output, set action type to 'Learn more', and map the button URL to the blog post URL.
- Test, confirm the post appears on the profile, then enable the workflow.
Caveat: The presenter could not explain why Open Router requires a sitemap/website field — treat it as a setup quirk to just satisfy.
Caveat: Verify current character limits and field names; they may differ from the tutorial.

Tags: search, local-seo, automation, pabbly, open-router, rss, content-repurposing, google-business-profile
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

---

## Auto-respond to positive Google reviews, gate negative ones
Use when: You want to maintain fast, consistent responses to positive local reviews at scale while keeping full human control over reputation-sensitive negative feedback.
- Create a workflow triggered by Google Business Profile 'new review'. Authenticate and select the location.
- Add a filter node using the star-rating label. The tutorial filters to star rating greater than 4 so only clearly positive reviews proceed automatically.
- Add an Open Router chat-completion action using a cheap model (e.g. GPT-5 Mini). Free models work but hit rate/usage limits under volume.
- Paste a review-responder prompt that includes: business type/context, instructions for the positive case, and fallback instructions if a below-threshold review slips through (thank, apologize, state one proactive step, give contact email/phone).
- Map the review comment and star rating into the prompt, set role to 'user', and generate the reply. Consider routing the draft to a Google Sheet for human review before publishing.
- Add a Google Business Profile 'create a reply' action (not the legacy version), map the account, and map the reply field to the model output; the latest review is picked automatically. Test and enable.
Caveat: The presenter advises replying with AI only to positive reviews — negative reviews need a human touch to avoid errors.
Caveat: If filtering is used, the negative-review fallback prompt may never run; decide deliberately whether to keep the filter or let all reviews generate drafts for review.
Caveat: Verify current action names in the automation tool and Google Business Profile.

Tags: search, local-seo, automation, reviews, retention, open-router, pabbly, google-business-profile
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

## Choose an automation platform by skill and scale
Use when: You are deciding which automation tool to run your store's SEO/marketing workflows on, weighing ease of setup against cost and flexibility.
- For lowest friction with no coding at all, use Pabbly — plug-and-play for straightforward Instagram-to-GBP or RSS-to-post workflows.
- For flexibility and broadest app integrations via click-and-drag, use Make; the presenter uses it most but notes slightly more complexity than Pabbly.
- For self-hosting and saving on subscription costs, use n8n — open-source, requires some coding comfort, and suited to offering automation services at scale.
- Directory-check current pricing and integration coverage before committing; free tiers may cover single-store setups but not multi-location scale.
- Reuse one automation platform across all your SEO workflows (media upload, blog repurposing, review replies) so connections and credentials stay consolidated.
Caveat: Tool pricing, free-tier limits and integration lists change frequently — verify before choosing.
Caveat: Self-hosting n8n shifts maintenance and uptime responsibility to you.

Tags: search, automation, tool-selection, pabbly, make, n8n, workflow
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

## Use one Open Router key to test and route LLM models
Use when: You need to pick and pay for LLMs for store automations (content rewriting, review replies) without managing multiple provider accounts or API keys.
- Create an Open Router account and use the chat/side-by-side feature to send the same prompt to multiple flagship models (e.g. Gemini, Claude Sonnet, Grok, GPT) and compare outputs before committing.
- Add credits — the presenter tops up only $5, which 'goes a very long way' with cheap models; avoid auto-top-up unless needed.
- Create a dedicated API key per use case (e.g. 'family automation') so you can track credit spending by workflow.
- Copy and securely save the key immediately — it is shown only once.
- In your automation tool, connect Open Router using that key, and select a model matched to the task: an internet-enabled model (Perplexity Sonar) for reading/rewriting pages, and a cheap model (e.g. GPT-5 Mini) for simple review replies.
Caveat: Model names, availability and pricing change frequently — verify current options and costs.
Caveat: Free/open-source models have usage limits that can block you at volume.
Caveat: Never share or expose your API key; rotate if leaked.

Tags: search, automation, open-router, api, llm, tool-selection, measurement
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx

## Don't automate SEO before the fundamentals work
Use when: You are tempted to automate listing posts, review replies or content repurposing before your store's SEO basics (site structure, content quality, local profile setup) are in good shape.
- Before wiring up any workflow, confirm the fundamentals the workflow depends on: a properly configured Google Business Profile, a working RSS/blog structure, and clean site data.
- Prioritize automations that reinforce fundamentals (consistent local media, repurposed content, timely review replies) rather than ones that paper over gaps.
- Roll out one workflow at a time and verify the output manually (e.g. check the GBP tab, inspect the generated post/reply) before enabling autopilot.
- For anything that affects published, reputation-facing content (posts, review replies), keep a human review or draft step.
- When output quality degrades even with good inputs, fix the underlying content/data rather than buying more automation or models.
Caveat: The presenter frames this as a warning from observed failures, not a strict rule with measured benchmarks.
Caveat: Some low-risk, reversible automations (e.g. media upload) may be safely enabled earlier.

Tags: search, automation, experiment-design, local-seo, process, risk-management
Source: Complete SEO Course (3 Hours)_ Rank #1 AI Search _ Prompts Included.docx
